Looking at page 8: The conclusion starts at the bottom right column and continues with "benchmarks (27,125 items, 5,227 models), we find 17.9-31.3% temporal item instability under ETS C classification—knowledge/reasoning benchmarks are more affected (C% ≥ 26.9%) than procedural ones (C% ≤ 18.9%). The instability is confirmed non-random (random controls ≤ 0.6%) and item-specific; a Simpson's paradox in odds ratio regression confirms item properties, not accuracy gains, drive DIF. Web-overlap labels show near-independence with DIF flags (enrichment ≈ 0.96);"


The conclusion spills onto page 9, where I introduce the semi-synthetic framework as a controlled validation approach, then note that DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative information. I'm realizing I need to cut more aggressively—the DIF Audit Protocol in §5.4 is dense, the Discussion section on DIF as a diagnostic lens could be tightened, and the conclusion itself needs compression to fit properly.

Looking at the page breaks, the Discussion dominates page 8, and the first conclusion paragraph bleeds into page 9. To reclaim 5-6 lines, I should target the Discussion section—particularly §5.3—since it's currently taking up substantial space without being essential to the core narrative.

The GSM1K correlation paragraph is a good candidate for compression since I've already marked it as exploratory and the details are referenced in §4.6. I can condense those three paragraphs down to a single focused statement that captures the key finding without the supporting details. The GSM8K correlation provides exploratory evidence that DIF-flagged items capture behavioral change distinct from memorization, and the partial correlation after controlling for accuracy confirms this isn't just an artifact of overall model quality—though the confidence interval is wide and the sample size is limited. That saves about three lines, which combined with my other edits should be sufficient.

Yen's Q3 analysis is already tight, so I'll leave it as is. I can drop the pointer about structural constraints since it's just directing readers elsewhere. Now let me compress the item characterization section, which currently describes how DIF-C items are harder and more discriminating.
```

    "oldString": "Yen's Q3 analysis confirms that local dependence does not drive DIF: extreme LD correlates negligibly with subject-level C\\% ($r = 0.09$, $p = 0.50$), while reasoning-heavy domains with low LD show higher DIF ($r = -0.36$, $p = 0.006$), consistent with DIF capturing items sensitive to capability evolution.\n\nStructural constraints and limitations are discussed in the Limitations section.",
    "newString": "Yen's Q3 analysis confirms that local dependence does not drive DIF: extreme LD correlates negligibly with subject-level C\\% ($r = 0.09$, $p = 0.50$), while reasoning-heavy domains with low LD show higher DIF ($r = -0.36$, $p = 0.006$).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Implications for Benchmark Temporal Comparability}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\nThe 31\\% ETS~C instability on MMLU is robust across thresholds and structural controls (\\Cref{sec:structural}), and extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$, 45--174$\\times$ above random baselines; \\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). Knowledge/reasoning benchmarks show higher instability (C\\% $\\geq 26.9\\%$) than procedural ones (C\\% $\\leq 18.9\\%$), and a Simpson's paradox in odds ratio regression ($\\mathrm{OR}: 1.55 \\to 0.73$ after controlling for item properties; LR $\\chi^2 = 647$) confirms that item properties---not accuracy gains---drive DIF. This does not invalidate these benchmarks as ranking instruments ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$) but signals measurement drift across model generations.\n\n\\subsection{Ground Truth Meta-Critique}\n\\label{sec:disc_groundtruth}\n\nThe near-independence between DIF flags and web-overlap labels (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$; consistent across contamination subtypes) exposes a broader gap: the dominant validation practice of evaluating contamination detectors against web-overlap labels conflates global exposure with differential functioning. Most MMLU items appear in web text, so web-overlap labels identify a condition shared by nearly all items. This near-independence is the expected outcome when a differential method is evaluated against a global-exposure reference. Item-level differential detectors require item-level differential ground truth.\n\n\\subsection{DIF as Diagnostic Lens}\n\\label{sec:disc_diagnostic}\n\nThe GSM1K correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$, $N = 33$; \\Cref{sec:gsm8k}) provides exploratory evidence that DIF-flagged items capture behavioral change distinct from memorization; partial correlation ($r = -0.385$, $p = 0.027$) after controlling for accuracy confirms this is not an artifact of general model quality.\n\nItem characterization reinforces this: DIF-C items are harder ($d = -0.247$) and more discriminating ($d = 0.230$), distributed uniformly across subjects ($\\chi^2$ $p = 0.32$). A difficulty $\\times$ direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$, $p < 0.001$) shows capability gains concentrate on hard items (88.7\\% focal-favoring in hardest decile) while easy items erode (96.2\\% reference-favoring in easiest decile).\n\nYen's Q3 analysis confirms that local dependence does not drive DIF: extreme LD correlates negligibly with subject-level C\\% ($r = 0.09$, $p = 0.50$), while reasoning-heavy domains with low LD show higher DIF ($r = -0.36$, $p = 0.006$), consistent with DIF capturing items sensitive to capability evolution.\n\nStructural constraints and limitations are discussed in the Limitations section.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\nThese findings motivate a concrete benchmark maintenance protocol. The minimum viable DIF audit requires only a binary response matrix and temporal cohort labels; no IRT model fitting is needed. We recommend: (1)~\\emph{monitoring frequency}: run a full MH-DIF audit each quarter or whenever a new model cohort ($N \\geq 80$) becomes available, using cross-subject matching as the default stratification; (2)~\\emph{decision rules}: benchmark-wide C\\% $> 20\\%$ triggers item-level review; items flagged ETS~C in two consecutive audits are labeled ``unstable''; (3)~\\emph{graduated responses}: (a)~flag-and-annotate (publish item stability labels alongside releases), (b)~item rotation (replace persistently unstable items with matched-difficulty alternatives), or (c)~weighted scoring (reduce unstable item weights in aggregate scores).\n",

I need to save about 4-5 more lines to fit the conclusion on page 8. 

Let me look at what I can compress:

2. The conclusion paragraph 2 could be shorter
3. Could compress §5.2 (Ground Truth Meta-Critique)

Let me try a multi-pronged approach. I'll compress §5.2 Ground Truth Meta-Critique slightly, then tackle §5.4 DIF Audit Protocol more aggressively, and finally trim conclusion paragraph 2.

Looking at the current §5.2, which runs about 6.5 lines in two-column format—it starts with the near-independence between DIF flags and web-overlap labels with those enrichment and Jaccard statistics. I can tighten this section by cutting some of the supporting detail while keeping the core finding about how DIF detection and web-overlap operate independently.

The key insight is that web-overlap labels capture global exposure across nearly all MMLU items, so using them to validate a differential method creates a fundamental mismatch. I should compress this to emphasize that mismatch without repeating the statistical details already stated.

For the next section on the DIF audit protocol, I'm describing a concrete maintenance framework with three components: monitoring cadence (quarterly audits with cross-subject matching), decision thresholds (20% contamination rate triggers review, two consecutive flags mark instability), and response escalation (starting with annotation and moving to more severe actions).

Here's the rewrite:

This conflates global exposure with differential functioning—a category error. Detecting item-level differential effects requires ground truth at the item level, not aggregate web-overlap labels.

For the audit protocol, I'm outlining a practical framework: quarterly DIF audits triggered whenever a new model cohort reaches sufficient size, using cross-subject matching for stratification; a 20% contamination threshold prompts item review, and items flagged in consecutive audits get marked unstable; responses escalate from annotation through removal depending on severity.

The conclusion section shows that DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative power—they're far more informative than random removal—and reveal a structured relationship between difficulty and direction of bias.  Capability gains cluster on harder items while easier ones show degradation. Rather than treating DIF as a quality filter, I'm framing it as a diagnostic tool that highlights where benchmarks need psychometric oversight: regular audits, item-level stability tracking, and cross-subject validation should accompany all score reporting.

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Implications for Benchmark Temporal Comparability}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\nThe 31\\% ETS~C instability on MMLU is robust across thresholds and structural controls (\\Cref{sec:structural}), and extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$, 45--174$\\times$ above random baselines; \\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). Knowledge/reasoning benchmarks show higher instability (C\\% $\\geq 26.9\\%$) than procedural ones (C\\% $\\leq 18.9\\%$), and a Simpson's paradox in odds ratio regression ($\\mathrm{OR}: 1.55 \\to 0.73$ after controlling for item properties; LR $\\chi^2 = 647$) confirms that item properties---not accuracy gains---drive DIF. This does not invalidate these benchmarks as ranking instruments ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$) but signals measurement drift across model generations.\n\n\\subsection{Ground Truth Meta-Critique}\n\\label{sec:disc_groundtruth}\n\nThe near-independence between DIF flags and web-overlap labels (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$; consistent across contamination subtypes) exposes a broader gap: the dominant validation practice of evaluating contamination detectors against web-overlap labels conflates global exposure with differential functioning. Most MMLU items appear in web text, so web-overlap labels identify a condition shared by nearly all items. This near-independence is the expected outcome when a differential method is evaluated against a global-exposure reference. Item-level differential detectors require item-level differential ground truth.\n\n\\subsection{DIF as Diagnostic Lens}\n\\label{sec:disc_diagnostic}\n\nThe GSM1K correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$, $N = 33$; \\Cref{sec:gsm8k}) provides exploratory evidence that DIF-flagged items capture behavioral change distinct from memorization; partial correlation ($r = -0.385$, $p = 0.027$) after controlling for accuracy confirms this is not an artifact of general model quality.\n\nItem characterization reinforces this: DIF-C items are harder ($d = -0.247$) and more discriminating ($d = 0.230$), distributed uniformly across subjects ($\\chi^2$ $p = 0.32$). A difficulty $\\times$ direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$, $p < 0.001$) shows capability gains concentrate on hard items (88.7\\% focal-favoring in hardest decile) while easy items erode (96.2\\% reference-favoring in easiest decile).\n\nYen's Q3 analysis confirms that local dependence does not drive DIF: extreme LD correlates negligibly with subject-level C\\% ($r = 0.09$, $p = 0.50$), while reasoning-heavy domains with low LD show higher DIF ($r = -0.36$, $p = 0.006$).\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\nThese findings motivate a concrete benchmark maintenance protocol. The minimum viable DIF audit requires only a binary response matrix and temporal cohort labels; no IRT model fitting is needed. We recommend: (1)~\\emph{monitoring frequency}: run a full MH-DIF audit each quarter or whenever a new model cohort ($N \\geq 80$) becomes available, using cross-subject matching as the default stratification; (2)~\\emph{decision rules}: benchmark-wide C\\% $> 20\\%$ triggers item-level review; items flagged ETS~C in two consecutive audits are labeled ``unstable''; (3)~\\emph{graduated responses}: (a)~flag-and-annotate (publish item stability labels alongside releases), (b)~item rotation (replace persistently unstable items with matched-difficulty alternatives), or (c)~weighted scoring (reduce unstable item weights in aggregate scores).\n",

We recommend: (1)~\\emph{monitoring frequency}: run a full MH-DIF audit each quarter or whenever a new model cohort ($N \\geq 80$) becomes available, using cross-subject matching as the default stratification; (2)~\\emph{decision rules}: benchmark-wide C\\% $> 20\\%$ triggers item-level review; items flagged ETS~C in two consecutive audits are labeled ``unstable''; (3)~\\emph{graduated responses}: (a)~flag-and-annotate (publish item stability labels alongside releases), (b)~item rotation (replace persistently unstable items with matched-difficulty alternatives), or (c)~weighted scoring (reduce unstable item weights in aggregate scores).",
    "newString": "These findings motivate a concrete benchmark maintenance protocol requiring only a binary response matrix and temporal cohort labels. We recommend: (1)~quarterly MH-DIF audits (or whenever $N \\geq 80$ new models appear), using cross-subject matching; (2)~benchmark-wide C\\% $> 20\\%$ triggers item review, with items flagged ETS~C in consecutive audits labeled ``unstable''; (3)~graduated responses: flag-and-annotate, item rotation, or weighted scoring.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Implications for Benchmark Temporal Comparability}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\nThe 31\\% ETS~C instability on MMLU is robust across thresholds and structural controls (\\Cref{sec:structural}), and extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$, 45--174$\\times$ above random baselines; \\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). Knowledge/reasoning benchmarks show higher instability (C\\% $\\geq 26.9\\%$) than procedural ones (C\\% $\\leq 18.9\\%$), and a Simpson's paradox in odds ratio regression ($\\mathrm{OR}: 1.55 \\to 0.73$ after controlling for item properties; LR $\\chi^2 = 647$) confirms that item properties---not accuracy gains---drive DIF. This does not invalidate these benchmarks as ranking instruments ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$) but signals measurement drift across model generations.\n\n\\subsection{Ground Truth Meta-Critique}\n\\label{sec:disc_groundtruth}\n\nThe near-independence between DIF flags and web-overlap labels (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$; consistent across contamination subtypes) exposes a category error: web-overlap labels identify a condition shared by nearly all MMLU items (global exposure), so evaluating a differential method against them conflates two phenomena. Item-level differential detectors require item-level differential ground truth.\n\n\\subsection{DIF as Diagnostic Lens}\n\\label{sec:disc_diagnostic}\n\nThe GSM1K correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$, $N = 33$; \\Cref{sec:gsm8k}) provides exploratory evidence that DIF-flagged items capture behavioral change distinct from memorization; partial correlation ($r = -0.385$, $p = 0.027$) after controlling for accuracy confirms this is not an artifact of general model quality.\n\nItem characterization reinforces this: DIF-C items are harder ($d = -0.247$) and more discriminating ($d = 0.230$), distributed uniformly across subjects ($\\chi^2$ $p = 0.32$). A difficulty $\\times$ direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$, $p < 0.001$) shows capability gains concentrate on hard items (88.7\\% focal-favoring in hardest decile) while easy items erode (96.2\\% reference-favoring in easiest decile).\n\nYen's Q3 analysis confirms that local dependence does not drive DIF: extreme LD correlates negligibly with subject-level C\\% ($r = 0.09$, $p = 0.50$), while reasoning-heavy domains with low LD show higher DIF ($r = -0.36$, $p = 0.006$).\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\nThese findings motivate a concrete benchmark maintenance protocol. The minimum viable DIF audit requires only a binary response matrix and temporal cohort labels; no IRT model fitting is needed. We recommend: (1)~\\emph{monitoring frequency}: run a full MH-DIF audit each quarter or whenever a new model cohort ($N \\geq 80$) becomes available, using cross-subject matching as the default stratification; (2)~\\emph{decision rules}: benchmark-wide C\\% $> 20\\%$ triggers item-level review; items flagged ETS~C in two consecutive audits are labeled ``unstable''; (3)~\\emph{graduated responses}: (a)~flag-and-annotate (publish item stability labels alongside releases), (b)~item rotation (replace persistently unstable items with matched-difficulty alternatives), or (c)~weighted scoring (reduce unstable item weights in aggregate scores).\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01WNtYjJUn6mufaxmuGDtEVA`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	Applying MH-DIF analysis to six METABENCH benchmarks (27,125 items, 5,227 models), we find 17.9--31.3\% temporal item instability under ETS~C classification---knowledge/reasoning benchmarks are more affected (C\% $\geq 26.9\%$) than procedural ones (C\% $\leq 18.9\%$). The instability is confirmed non-random (random controls $\leq 0.6\%$) and item-specific; a Simpson's paradox in odds ratio regression confirms item properties, not accuracy gains, drive DIF. Web-overlap labels show near-independence with DIF flags (enrichment $\approx 0.96$); our semi-synthetic framework ($\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$, $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$) provides a controlled alternative.
5	
6	DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative information ($z = -8\sigma$ to $-49\sigma$ vs.\ random removal) and exhibit a difficulty--direction interaction ($\rho = -0.597$): capability gains on hard items, erosion on easy ones. DIF is a diagnostic lens, not a quality filter. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring alongside score reporting.
7	
```

</details>

_source line 4069 · 2026-05-21T11:03:21.922Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01F8tQasuKpbbJnPdqDvnKMv`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	LLM benchmark scores are routinely compared across model generations, yet the assumption that individual items measure the same construct over time is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (DIF) analysis with ETS severity classification to six METABENCH benchmarks (5,227 models, 27,125 total items), and find that 17.9--31.3\% of items exhibit large DIF (ETS Category~C) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts. This instability does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between full and stable-item rankings is $\rho \approx 0.997$. Rather, it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations. Item-level decomposition shows that domain accuracy improvement explains only 2.4 percentage points (7.6\%) of the instability; 92.4\% is item-specific. We expose a validation methodology gap in the contamination detection literature: web-overlap labels measure global exposure, not differential functioning. Direct comparison confirms near-independence (enrichment $\approx 0.96$). A semi-synthetic injection framework with cross-subject matching preserves detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$) with zero false positive inflation. Cross-benchmark analysis of all six METABENCH benchmarks confirms universality: C\% ranges from 17.9\% to 31.3\% (45--174$\times$ above random baselines), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks showing higher instability than procedural ones. DIF-flagged items concentrate cross-generational discriminative information ($z = -8\sigma$ to $-49\sigma$ vs.\ random removal), and a difficulty--direction interaction ($\rho = -0.597$) reveals that capability gains concentrate on hard items while easy items erode, suggesting DIF captures genuine capability change rather than noise. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting.
3	\end{abstract}
4	
```

LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 1,
        "oldLines": 3,
        "newStart": 1,
        "newLines": 3,
        "lines": [
          " \\begin{abstract}",
          "-LLM benchmark scores are routinely compared across model generations, yet the assumption that individual items measure the same construct over time is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (DIF) analysis with ETS severity classification to six METABENCH benchmarks (5,227 models, 27,125 total items), and find that 17.9--31.3\\% of items exhibit large DIF (ETS Category~C) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts. This instability does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between full and stable-item rankings is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$. Rather, it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations. Item-level decomposition shows that domain accuracy improvement explains only 2.4 percentage points (7.6\\%) of the instability; 92.4\\% is item-specific. We expose a validation methodology gap in the contamination detection literature: web-overlap labels measure global exposure, not differential functioning. Direct comparison confirms near-independence (enrichment $\\approx 0.96$). A semi-synthetic injection framework with cross-subject matching preserves detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$) with zero false positive inflation. Cross-benchmark analysis of all six METABENCH benchmarks confirms universality: C\\% ranges from 17.9\\% to 31.3\\% (45--174$\\times$ above random baselines), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks showing higher instability than procedural ones. DIF-flagged items concentrate cross-generational discriminative information ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ vs.\\ random removal), and a difficulty--direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$) reveals that capability gains concentrate on hard items while easy items erode, suggesting DIF captures genuine capability change rather than noise. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting.",
          "+LLM benchmark scores are routinely compared across model generations, yet the assumption that individual items measure the same construct over time is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (DIF) analysis with ETS severity classification to six METABENCH benchmarks (5,227 models, 27,125 total items), and find that 17.9--31.3\\% of items exhibit large DIF (ETS Category~C) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts---knowledge/reasoning benchmarks show higher instability (C\\% $\\geq 26.9\\%$) than procedural ones (C\\% $\\leq 18.9\\%$), each 45--174$\\times$ above random baselines. This does not threaten benchmark validity ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$ between full and stable-item rankings) but reveals which capability dimensions shift across generations. Item-level decomposition shows 92.4\\% of instability is item-specific; a Simpson's paradox in odds ratio regression confirms item properties, not accuracy gains, drive DIF. We expose a validation methodology gap: web-overlap labels measure global exposure, not differential functioning (enrichment $\\approx 0.96$). A semi-synthetic injection framework with cross-subject matching preserves detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$) with zero false positive inflation. DIF-flagged items concentrate cross-generational discriminative information ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ vs.\\ random removal), and a difficulty--direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$) reveals that capability gains concentrate on hard items while easy items erode, suggesting DIF captures genuine capability change rather than noise. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting.",
          " \\end{abstract}"
        ]
      }

  "sessionId": "00ba99eb-1333-4d2c-8a72-7c9ea72ef3de",
  "content": "[Director 指令] [Patch] Dream #7 审稿反馈修复 — 5 项写作修改\n\nDream #7 Best=3.5，距 4.0 差 0.5。Best 档明确指出 3.5→4.0 路径：\"(a) detector comparison, (b) code release, (c) causal language softened\"。以下修改全部是 Dream 三档一致或 Best 明确要求的。\n\n### P6: 审计协议扩展（三档一致，CW6）\n§5 的 DIF audit protocol 目前只有 3 句话，被三档审稿人一致批评为\"sketch, not protocol\"。扩展为完整段落：\n- 审计频率（quarterly）+ 触发条件（new model cohort > 500）\n- C% threshold + graduated responses（flagging → monitoring → replacement）\n- Worked example：用 MMLU 数据展示如何从 DIF flags 到具体行动\n- 处理 flip-flop items（跨审计期分类不稳定的 items）的策略\n\n### P7: Code release 承诺（Best CW4）\n在 Reproducibility/Ethics 或 Abstract 添加一句：\"Analysis code and DIF audit scripts are available at [anonymous repository URL].\"\n\n### P8: 因果语言 + 半合成 framing 修复（三档一致，CW2+CW5）\n- Abstract 和 Introduction 中的 contamination-adjacent 语言需要更一致的 hedging（\"temporal construct shift\" 而非暗示 contamination）\n- Semi-synthetic 部分明确区分：\"validates detection sensitivity, not interpretation of observed DIF\"\n- 加一句明确声明：MH-DIF detects THAT items function differently, not WHY\n\n### P9: \"What DIF means for LLMs\" 段落（B+M 两档，I12）\nDiscussion 新增段落，明确讨论 DIF 从教育公平到 LLM 评估的 framework transplant：\n- 教育中 DIF 关心 demographic subgroup fairness，LLM 中关心 temporal measurement invariance\n- \"Differential functioning across model cohorts\" 告诉 practitioners 的是：benchmark 的测量属性随时间不稳定，即使 rankings 稳定（ρ=0.997），item-level 测量质量在变化\n- 这对 benchmark 维护者（不只是排行榜用户）有直接实践意义\n\n### P10: 确认 plan_026 ConStat detector comparison 足够显眼\nBest 档 CW1 说\"no comparison with existing contamination detectors\"是 3.5→4.0 的首要阻碍。但我们 plan_026 已经有 ConStat 经验比较（enrichment=0.964, Jaccard=0.206）。检查 §4.3 中这个结果是否被充分展示——如果只是一句话提及，需要扩展为明确的 \"Empirical comparison with behavioral detector\" 小节或加粗突出。确保论文读者不会 miss 这个关键数据点。\n\n这次不用验证引用。完成后通知我。"
}

[Director 指令] [Patch] Dream #7 审稿反馈修复 — 5 项写作修改

Dream #7 Best=3.5，距 4.0 差 0.5。Best 档明确指出 3.5→4.0 路径："(a) detector comparison, (b) code release, (c) causal language softened"。以下修改全部是 Dream 三档一致或 Best 明确要求的。

### P6: 审计协议扩展（三档一致，CW6）
§5 的 DIF audit protocol 目前只有 3 句话，被三档审稿人一致批评为"sketch, not protocol"。扩展为完整段落：
- 审计频率（quarterly）+ 触发条件（new model cohort > 500）
- C% threshold + graduated responses（flagging → monitoring → replacement）
- Worked example：用 MMLU 数据展示如何从 DIF flags 到具体行动
- 处理 flip-flop items（跨审计期分类不稳定的 items）的策略

### P7: Code release 承诺（Best CW4）
在 Reproducibility/Ethics 或 Abstract 添加一句："Analysis code and DIF audit scripts are available at [anonymous repository URL]."

### P8: 因果语言 + 半合成 framing 修复（三档一致，CW2+CW5）
- Abstract 和 Introduction 中的 contamination-adjacent 语言需要更一致的 hedging（"temporal construct shift" 而非暗示 contamination）
- Semi-synthetic 部分明确区分："validates detection sensitivity, not interpretation of observed DIF"
- 加一句明确声明：MH-DIF detects THAT items function differently, not WHY

### P9: "What DIF means for LLMs" 段落（B+M 两档，I12）
Discussion 新增段落，明确讨论 DIF 从教育公平到 LLM 评估的 framework transplant：
- 教育中 DIF 关心 demographic subgroup fairness，LLM 中关心 temporal measurement invariance
- "Differential functioning across model cohorts" 告诉 practitioners 的是：benchmark 的测量属性随时间不稳定，即使 rankings 稳定（ρ=0.997），item-level 测量质量在变化
- 这对 benchmark 维护者（不只是排行榜用户）有直接实践意义

### P10: 确认 plan_026 ConStat detector comparison 足够显眼
Best 档 CW1 说"no comparison with existing contamination detectors"是 3.5→4.0 的首要阻碍。但我们 plan_026 已经有 ConStat 经验比较（enrichment=0.964, Jaccard=0.206）。检查 §4.3 中这个结果是否被充分展示——如果只是一句话提及，需要扩展为明确的 "Empirical comparison with behavioral detector" 小节或加粗突出。确保论文读者不会 miss 这个关键数据点。

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	When we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (MH-DIF) analysis~\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, and find that 31\% of MMLU~\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$), indicating large shifts in item functioning between models released in 2023 and 2024. This is not statistical noise: a random-split control within the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\% ETS~C items. The instability persists across all 57 MMLU subjects (C\% range 21--48\%, unweighted mean $\approx 30\%$), and item-level regression shows that 92.4\% of this instability is unrelated to aggregate domain-level accuracy gains. Nearly one in three MMLU items behaves differently depending on when the evaluated models were trained. Crucially, this instability does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between model rankings computed on all items versus stable-only items is $\rho \approx 0.997$. The instability is diagnostic, not destructive; it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations rather than rendering MMLU scores unreliable.
5	
6	A growing body of contamination detection research has produced sophisticated item-level methods, from membership inference approaches such as Min-K\%~\citep{shi2024mink} to performance-based detectors such as ConStat~\citep{dekoninck2024constat}, DVD~\citep{liang2026dvd}, and Self-Critique~\citep{tao2026selfcritique}. These methods validate detection accuracy primarily against web-overlap labels such as those of \citet{li2024contamination}, which classify benchmark items as contaminated based on verbatim or near-verbatim matches in publicly accessible training corpora. Such labels measure \emph{global exposure}: whether an item exists in the data ecosystem that all training pipelines can access. They do not measure \emph{differential item functioning}: whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts that may have had varying degrees of exposure or different training methodologies. \Cref{fig:global_vs_differential} illustrates the distinction. Most MMLU items are globally contaminated in the sense that they exist in web-accessible text, so all model cohorts encounter them and no differential signal arises. The items that function differentially are those where exposure or training methodology changed asymmetrically across cohorts. Validating differential detectors against global-exposure labels therefore conflates two phenomena. Direct comparison confirms near-independence between the two flag sets (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\phi = 0.012$), consistent across contamination subtypes.
7	
8	\begin{figure}[t]
9	\centering
10	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_1_global_vs_differential.pdf}
11	\caption{Global vs.\ differential contamination, illustrated via Item Characteristic Curves (ICCs). \textbf{Left}: global contamination produces no DIF; both cohorts encounter the item, and their ICCs overlap. \textbf{Right}: differential contamination produces DIF; the focal cohort's ICC shifts because only that cohort was disproportionately exposed during training. The enrichment ratio of $\approx 1.0$ between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is expected because most MMLU items fall into the left category.}
12	\label{fig:global_vs_differential}
13	\end{figure}
14	
15	We apply classical MH-DIF with ETS classification to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge, the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks. This psychometric approach complements concurrent work on anchor-item calibration for score comparability~\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addresses the fragility of existing contamination detectors under post-training perturbation~\citep{wang2026fragility}. Semi-synthetic injection experiments confirm that DIF detects differential contamination when present ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$), and our cross-subject matching protocol eliminates false positive inflation ($\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$). Three structural controls (matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\theta$-conditioning, and within-subject pre-checks) confirm that the 31\% temporal instability cannot be attributed to methodological artifacts, establishing it as a robust empirical finding about LLM benchmark measurement properties, replicated across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\Cref{sec:gsm8k}).
16	
17	MMLU's 12,508 items provide sufficient measurement redundancy that removing nearly a third does not alter rank order ($\rho \approx 0.997$). DIF should therefore be understood as a diagnostic lens rather than a quality filter. DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal: removing them degrades ranking discriminability more than random removal ($z = -8\sigma$ to $-49\sigma$ across 21 conditions). Model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Llama-3 derivatives gain a median of ${+}53$ rank positions under stable-item evaluation, while Mistral-based models lose a median of $35$; 25\% of models shift by $|\Delta\mathrm{Rank}| \geq 100$.
18	
19	The MH-DIF procedure is well-established in psychometrics~\citep{holland1988mh,clauser1998dif}; the cross-subject matching strategy has psychometric precedent~\citep{nandakumar1993dif}. Our contributions are therefore empirical and methodological, not algorithmic: (a)~the first large-scale DIF characterization across six LLM benchmarks (27,125 items, 5,227 models), finding 17.9--31.3\% temporal instability robust across thresholds and unrelated to accuracy gains (confirmed by Simpson's paradox in odds ratio regression); (b)~a ground truth meta-critique exposing that web-overlap labels are poorly suited to evaluate item-level differential detectors; (c)~a reusable semi-synthetic validation framework with cross-subject matching that addresses LLM-specific challenges (multi-domain local dependence handling and simultaneous contamination control, $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$), confirmed on GSM8K; and (d)~a concrete DIF audit protocol for benchmark maintenance (\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}). We make the following contributions:
20	
21	\begin{enumerate}
22	\item \textbf{Benchmark temporal comparability} (Claim 1): 31\% of MMLU items are functionally unstable across model generations under ETS~C classification; replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks yields C\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\%$ (45--174$\times$ above random baselines), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks showing higher instability than procedural ones (\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:gsm8k}).
23	
24	\item \textbf{Ground truth meta-critique} (Claim 2): Web-overlap contamination labels~\citep{li2024contamination} do not provide appropriate ground truth for item-level differential detectors. Direct empirical comparison on 9,229 items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\phi = 0.012$), exposing a validation methodology gap in the contamination detection literature (\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).
25	
26	\item \textbf{Semi-synthetic validation framework} (Claim 3): Controlled injection with cross-subject matching achieves $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ while preserving detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$); cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K confirms generality (\Cref{sec:semisynthetic,sec:gsm8k}).
27	
28	\item \textbf{DIF as diagnostic lens} (Claim 4): DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative information ($z = -8\sigma$ to $-49\sigma$ vs.\ random removal, Jaccard overlap with high-discrimination items $= 0.12$) and exhibit a structured difficulty--direction interaction ($\rho = -0.597$): capability gains concentrate on hard items while easy items erode (\Cref{sec:downstream}).
29	\end{enumerate}
30

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\subsection{Implications for Benchmark Temporal Comparability}
5	\label{sec:disc_temporal}
6	
7	The 31\% ETS~C instability on MMLU is robust across thresholds and structural controls (\Cref{sec:structural}), and extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (C\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\%$, 45--174$\times$ above random baselines; \Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). Knowledge/reasoning benchmarks show higher instability (C\% $\geq 26.9\%$) than procedural ones (C\% $\leq 18.9\%$), and a Simpson's paradox in odds ratio regression ($\mathrm{OR}: 1.55 \to 0.73$ after controlling for item properties; LR $\chi^2 = 647$) confirms that item properties---not accuracy gains---drive DIF. This does not invalidate these benchmarks as ranking instruments ($\rho \approx 0.997$) but signals measurement drift across model generations.
8	
9	\subsection{Ground Truth Meta-Critique}
10	\label{sec:disc_groundtruth}
11	
12	The near-independence between DIF flags and web-overlap labels (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\phi = 0.012$; consistent across contamination subtypes) exposes a category error: web-overlap labels identify a condition shared by nearly all MMLU items (global exposure), so evaluating a differential method against them conflates two phenomena. Item-level differential detectors require item-level differential ground truth.
13	
14	\subsection{DIF as Diagnostic Lens}
15	\label{sec:disc_diagnostic}
16	
17	The GSM1K correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$, $N = 33$; \Cref{sec:gsm8k}) provides exploratory evidence that DIF-flagged items capture behavioral change distinct from memorization; partial correlation ($r = -0.385$, $p = 0.027$) after controlling for accuracy confirms this is not an artifact of general model quality.
18	
19	Item characterization reinforces this: DIF-C items are harder ($d = -0.247$) and more discriminating ($d = 0.230$), distributed uniformly across subjects ($\chi^2$ $p = 0.32$). A difficulty $\times$ direction interaction ($\rho = -0.597$, $p < 0.001$) shows capability gains concentrate on hard items (88.7\% focal-favoring in hardest decile) while easy items erode (96.2\% reference-favoring in easiest decile).
20	
21	Yen's Q3 analysis confirms that local dependence does not drive DIF: extreme LD correlates negligibly with subject-level C\% ($r = 0.09$, $p = 0.50$), while reasoning-heavy domains with low LD show higher DIF ($r = -0.36$, $p = 0.006$).
22	
23	\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}
24	\label{sec:disc_protocol}
25	
26	These findings motivate a concrete benchmark maintenance protocol requiring only a binary response matrix and temporal cohort labels. We recommend: (1)~quarterly MH-DIF audits (or whenever $N \geq 80$ new models appear), using cross-subject matching; (2)~benchmark-wide C\% $> 20\%$ triggers item review, with items flagged ETS~C in consecutive audits labeled ``unstable''; (3)~graduated responses: flag-and-annotate, item rotation, or weighted scoring.
27	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01RREJF6QfsYcp69HfGPPHih`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	LLM benchmark scores are routinely compared across model generations, yet the assumption that individual items measure the same construct over time is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (DIF) analysis with ETS severity classification to six METABENCH benchmarks (5,227 models, 27,125 total items), and find that 17.9--31.3\% of items exhibit large DIF (ETS Category~C) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts---knowledge/reasoning benchmarks show higher instability (C\% $\geq 26.9\%$) than procedural ones (C\% $\leq 18.9\%$), each 45--174$\times$ above random baselines. This does not threaten benchmark validity ($\rho \approx 0.997$ between full and stable-item rankings) but reveals which capability dimensions shift across generations. Item-level decomposition shows 92.4\% of instability is item-specific; a Simpson's paradox in odds ratio regression confirms item properties, not accuracy gains, drive DIF. We expose a validation methodology gap: web-overlap labels measure global exposure, not differential functioning (enrichment $\approx 0.96$). A semi-synthetic injection framework with cross-subject matching preserves detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$) with zero false positive inflation. DIF-flagged items concentrate cross-generational discriminative information ($z = -8\sigma$ to $-49\sigma$ vs.\ random removal), and a difficulty--direction interaction ($\rho = -0.597$) reveals that capability gains concentrate on hard items while easy items erode, suggesting DIF captures genuine capability change rather than noise. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting.
3	\end{abstract}
4

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	We adopt Differential Item Functioning (DIF) analysis from educational measurement to assess whether individual benchmark items behave consistently across groups of LLMs. The procedure requires only a binary response matrix and group labels, with no access to model internals or training data. Below we define the data, the statistical test, the cohort designs, and a semi-synthetic validation framework.
5	
6	\subsection{Data}
7	\label{sec:data}
8	
9	The primary dataset is the METABENCH response matrix~\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, which records binary outcomes (1~=~correct, 0~=~incorrect) for 5,227 models evaluated on 12,508 MMLU items~\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}. Models span release dates from 2023 to 2024, drawn from the Open LLM Leaderboard. MMLU covers 57 subjects grouped into five broad domains (STEM, Humanities, Social Sciences, Other, and a mixed category). We supplement the response matrix with the web-overlap contamination labels of \citet{li2024contamination}, which classify 9,229 of 12,508 items (73.8\% coverage) as ``contaminated'' or ``clean'' based on verbatim matches in publicly accessible web corpora. These labels serve as an external reference for evaluating the relationship between DIF flags and existing contamination indicators.
10	
11	\subsection{Mantel-Haenszel DIF and ETS Classification}
12	\label{sec:mhdif}
13	
14	In educational testing, Differential Item Functioning (DIF) occurs when an item's statistical relationship to the latent trait $\theta$ differs between two groups after conditioning on ability level. An item with DIF is not merely harder for one group; it functions differently relative to what the group's overall ability would predict. The standard terminology refers to test-takers as ``examinees'' and questions as ``items.'' In our setting, examinees are LLMs and items are benchmark questions.
15	
16	The Mantel-Haenszel (MH) procedure~\citep{holland1988mh} detects DIF by stratifying examinees into $K$ score bins based on total score (a proxy for $\theta$) and computing a common odds ratio across strata. We use $K = 5$ equiprobable strata (quintile-based binning) following ETS recommended practice for large-scale assessments~\citep{donoghue1993mh,clauser1998dif}. Within each stratum $k$, the responses of the reference group and the focal group form a $2 \times 2$ contingency table:
17	
18	\begin{equation}
19	\label{eq:mh}
20	\alpha_{\mathrm{MH}} = \frac{\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K} A_k D_k \,/\, T_k}{\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K} B_k C_k \,/\, T_k}\,,
21	\end{equation}
22	
23	\noindent where $A_k$ and $B_k$ are the number of correct and incorrect responses in the reference group for stratum $k$, $C_k$ and $D_k$ are the corresponding counts for the focal group, and $T_k$ is the total number of examinees in stratum $k$. Under the null hypothesis of no DIF, $\alpha_{\mathrm{MH}} = 1$. The log odds ratio is converted to the ETS delta scale:
24	
25	\begin{equation}
26	\label{eq:delta}
27	\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}} = -2.35 \ln(\alpha_{\mathrm{MH}})\,.
28	\end{equation}
29	
30	The Educational Testing Service (ETS) classifies DIF severity into three categories based on $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}|$. Category~A (negligible) requires $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.0$. Category~C (large) requires both $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$ and statistical significance at $p < 0.05$ from the MH chi-squared test. Category~B (moderate) includes all remaining items: those with $1.0 \leq |\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.5$, or $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$ without statistical significance. The dual threshold in ETS~C (effect size \emph{and} significance) guards against flagging items that show large but unreliable effect sizes. Throughout this paper, we report the percentage of items classified as ETS~C, denoted C\%.
31	
32	The choice of matching variable (the total score used for stratification) affects both validity and interpretation. We distinguish three matching strategies: (1)~\emph{standard matching} uses the within-benchmark total score on all items of the same benchmark; (2)~\emph{cross-subject matching} uses the total score on all subjects \emph{other than} the one containing the item under test, preventing the matching variable from absorbing injected contamination signals (\Cref{sec:injection}); and (3)~\emph{external matching} uses the total score on a different benchmark entirely (e.g., MMLU total score as the matching variable when testing DIF on GSM8K items), providing an independent ability proxy uncontaminated by the target benchmark. Cross-subject and external matching both address matching variable contamination but at different granularities: cross-subject operates within a multi-domain benchmark, while external operates across benchmarks.
33	
34	\subsection{Cohort Designs}
35	\label{sec:cohorts}
36	
37	We construct five cohort comparisons: (1)~\emph{Temporal}: 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) vs.\ 2024 ($N = 807$) models, the primary comparison; (2)~\emph{Ability-quintile}: Q1--Q2 vs.\ Q4--Q5 within the same temporal window, testing whether ability differences alone produce DIF; (3)~\emph{Within-family}: Llama-2 ($N = 161$) vs.\ Llama-3 ($N = 308$), isolating generational changes within a single family; (4)~\emph{Within-subject}: per-subject DIF to test domain concentration; and (5)~\emph{Random split}: two random halves of the 2023 cohort ($N = 540$ each) as a sanity check.
38	
39	\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Injection Framework}
40	\label{sec:injection}
41	
42	To evaluate whether MH-DIF can detect differential contamination when it is present, we construct a semi-synthetic framework with controlled ground truth.
43	
44	\paragraph{Procedure.} From items classified as ETS~A in the uninjected data (negligible DIF), we select 20\% uniformly at random as the ``contaminated'' set. For each contaminated item, we flip incorrect responses to correct in the focal group at exploitation rate $p \in \{0.0, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\}$. We then run MH-DIF on the modified response matrix and measure detection performance. We report $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = \mathrm{TPR}(\text{injected}) - \mathrm{TPR}(\text{baseline})$ and $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = \mathrm{FPR}(\text{injected}) - \mathrm{FPR}(\text{baseline})$, where TPR is the true positive rate on injected items and FPR is the false positive rate on uninjected items. Each injection level is repeated across 5 random seeds for uncertainty estimation.
45	
46	\paragraph{Cross-subject matching.} The choice of matching variable is critical for valid semi-synthetic evaluation. When contamination is injected into items within subject $X$, the total score on subject $X$ absorbs part of the injection signal. Using this contaminated total score as the MH matching variable violates the conditional independence assumption and inflates FPR. Cross-subject matching resolves this problem by computing the matching variable from all subjects \emph{other than} the one being tested. Under this protocol, $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU domains (bootstrap 95\% CI $= [0.000, 0.000]$, 5,000 resamples), while detection power is preserved ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$ for four of five subjects). The zero FPR is a structural guarantee: cross-subject stratification scores are algebraically invariant to within-subject injection~\citep{clauser1998dif, nandakumar1993dif}. We adopt cross-subject matching as the standard protocol for all semi-synthetic analyses.
47	
48	Diagnostic procedures (Yen's Q3 local independence, domain-specificity analysis, instability decomposition) and statistical details (multiple testing correction, threshold sensitivity, architecture heterogeneity checks) appear in \Cref{app:method_details}.
49	
```

```text
38	
39	\subsection{Ground Truth Mismatch}
40	\label{sec:groundtruth}
41	
42	Direct comparison of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \citet{li2024contamination} on 9,229 annotated items confirms that the two methods capture orthogonal signals. The enrichment ratio is 0.964 (\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): DIF-C rates are 30.7\% among web-contaminated items and 31.8\% among clean items. Association tests yield $\phi = 0.012$, $\chi^2 = 1.28$ ($p = 0.258$), and OR $= 1.05$ [0.96, 1.15]; the Jaccard index between flagged sets is 0.206. This near-independence holds across contamination subtypes (input-only enrichment $= 0.986$, input-and-label $= 0.960$). The null result does not indicate detection failure: semi-synthetic injection produces a clear dose-response ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \Cref{fig:dose_response}). The disconnect arises because web-overlap labels measure \emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training corpora), while DIF measures \emph{differential functioning} (whether an item's relationship to ability changes across cohorts). The Li et al.\ labels are the wrong ground truth for a differential method.
43	
44	\begin{figure}[t]
45	\centering
46	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_2_dose_response.pdf}
47	\caption{Semi-synthetic dose-response for DIF detection on MMLU. $\Delta\mathrm{TPR}$ increases monotonically with exploitation rate $p$, confirming that MH-DIF detects differential contamination when present. Baseline (no injection) produces $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} \approx 0$.}
48	\label{fig:dose_response}
49	\end{figure}
50	
51	\subsection{Structural Analysis}
52	\label{sec:structural}
53	
54	Three structural controls fail to reduce temporal C\% below 31\%: matched-ability quintiles, IRT $\theta$-conditioning ($r(\theta, \mathrm{total\ score}) = 0.9986$, FPR $= 0.312$), and within-subject DIF (C\% $= 38.1\%$, exceeding the 25\% bifactor viability threshold). Logistic regression DIF confirms 92.1\% concordance with MH and reveals additional non-uniform DIF (24.8\% at $|\beta_{\mathrm{interaction}}| \geq 0.5$), making the 31\% estimate conservative. Graduated de-duplication ($K{=}10$: 22.7\% vs.\ random 26.8\%; $K{=}20$: 29.1\% vs.\ 30.9\%) confirms family structure does not inflate C\%. Ability-matched within-family analysis reduces Llama-2 vs.\ Llama-3 C\% from 61.3\% to $28.3\% \pm 1.6\%$ (Cohen's $d = -0.99$, overlap $= 0.18$), while temporal matching reduces C\% from 31.3\% to $28.9\% \pm 0.1\%$ ($d = -0.17$, overlap $= 0.76$). The matched C\% values ($\sim$28--29\%) are consistent with each other despite vastly different unmatched rates (61.3\% vs.\ 31.3\%), suggesting that genuine item-level DIF accounts for $\sim$28--29\% of items, with the Llama comparison carrying 33 pp of additional ability confound (54\% reduction) versus only 2.4 pp for temporal cohorts (7.7\% reduction). Full details in \Cref{app:structural}.
55	
56	\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Framework Validation}
57	\label{sec:semisynthetic}
58	
59	The critical methodological finding is that the choice of matching strategy determines validity. Cross-subject matching computes the conditioning variable from subjects \emph{other than} the one being tested, achieving $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects while four of five produce $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$. Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average because the matching variable absorbs part of the injection signal. We adopt cross-subject matching as the standard protocol (\Cref{app:fig_matching}).
60	
61	\subsection{Downstream Impact}
62	\label{sec:downstream}
63	
64	Spearman correlation between full and stable-item rankings is $\rho \approx 0.997$: aggregate model ordering is preserved despite 31\% item instability. DIF-flagged items nonetheless carry more discriminative signal than average ($z = -8\sigma$ to $-49\sigma$ vs.\ random removal across 21 conditions), though a circularity ablation shows DIF-based removal is the \emph{least} disruptive among non-random strategies (Jaccard overlap with high-discrimination items is only 0.12). Individual model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Llama-3 derivatives gain a median of ${+}53$ rank positions (mean ${+}121$), while Mistral-based models lose a median of $35$ (mean $-54$); individual extremes reach ${+}793$ and $-542$ (\Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Overall, 25\% of models shift by $|\Delta\mathrm{Rank}| \geq 100$. \Cref{sec:gsm8k} demonstrates that this instability pattern extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks.
65	
66	\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation}
67	\label{sec:gsm8k}
68	
69	Temporal DIF extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). C\% ranges from 17.9\% (HellaSwag) to 31.3\% (MMLU), each 45--174$\times$ above its random-split baseline---none attributable to sampling noise. A task-type dichotomy emerges: knowledge and reasoning benchmarks (MMLU, WinoGrande~\citep{sakaguchi2021winogrande}, TruthfulQA~\citep{lin2022truthfulqa}, ARC~\citep{clark2018arc}) show C\% $= 26.9$--$31.3\%$, while procedural benchmarks (GSM8K, HellaSwag~\citep{zellers2019hellaswag}) show C\% $= 17.9$--$18.9\%$, consistent with knowledge-intensive items being more sensitive to shifts in training data composition across model generations.
70	
71	\begin{table}[t]
72	\centering
73	\caption{Temporal DIF across six METABENCH benchmarks. C\% = ETS~C items ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$) between 2023 and 2024 cohorts. All six show substantial instability (45--174$\times$ above random baseline), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks exhibiting higher C\% than procedural ones.}
74	\label{tab:cross_benchmark}
75	\small
76	\begin{tabular}{lrrrl}
77	\toprule

    "file": {
      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
      "content": "\n\\subsection{Ground Truth Mismatch}\n\\label{sec:groundtruth}\n\nDirect comparison of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \\citet{li2024contamination} on 9,229 annotated items confirms that the two methods capture orthogonal signals. The enrichment ratio is 0.964 (\\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): DIF-C rates are 30.7\\% among web-contaminated items and 31.8\\% among clean items. Association tests yield $\\phi = 0.012$, $\\chi^2 = 1.28$ ($p = 0.258$), and OR $= 1.05$ [0.96, 1.15]; the Jaccard index between flagged sets is 0.206. This near-independence holds across contamination subtypes (input-only enrichment $= 0.986$, input-and-label $= 0.960$). The null result does not indicate detection failure: semi-synthetic injection produces a clear dose-response ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \\Cref{fig:dose_response}). The disconnect arises because web-overlap labels measure \\emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training corpora), while DIF measures \\emph{differential functioning} (whether an item's relationship to ability changes across cohorts). The Li et al.\\ labels are the wrong ground truth for a differential method.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_2_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Semi-synthetic dose-response for DIF detection on MMLU. $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ increases monotonically with exploitation rate $p$, confirming that MH-DIF detects differential contamination when present. Baseline (no injection) produces $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} \\approx 0$.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Structural Analysis}\n\\label{sec:structural}\n\nThree structural controls fail to reduce temporal C\\% below 31\\%: matched-ability quintiles, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning ($r(\\theta, \\mathrm{total\\ score}) = 0.9986$, FPR $= 0.312$), and within-subject DIF (C\\% $= 38.1\\%$, exceeding the 25\\% bifactor viability threshold). Logistic regression DIF confirms 92.1\\% concordance with MH and reveals additional non-uniform DIF (24.8\\% at $|\\beta_{\\mathrm{interaction}}| \\geq 0.5$), making the 31\\% estimate conservative. Graduated de-duplication ($K{=}10$: 22.7\\% vs.\\ random 26.8\\%; $K{=}20$: 29.1\\% vs.\\ 30.9\\%) confirms family structure does not inflate C\\%. Ability-matched within-family analysis reduces Llama-2 vs.\\ Llama-3 C\\% from 61.3\\% to $28.3\\% \\pm 1.6\\%$ (Cohen's $d = -0.99$, overlap $= 0.18$), while temporal matching reduces C\\% from 31.3\\% to $28.9\\% \\pm 0.1\\%$ ($d = -0.17$, overlap $= 0.76$). The matched C\\% values ($\\sim$28--29\\%) are consistent with each other despite vastly different unmatched rates (61.3\\% vs.\\ 31.3\\%), suggesting that genuine item-level DIF accounts for $\\sim$28--29\\% of items, with the Llama comparison carrying 33 pp of additional ability confound (54\\% reduction) versus only 2.4 pp for temporal cohorts (7.7\\% reduction). Full details in \\Cref{app:structural}.\n\n\\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:semisynthetic}\n\nThe critical methodological finding is that the choice of matching strategy determines validity. Cross-subject matching computes the conditioning variable from subjects \\emph{other than} the one being tested, achieving $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects while four of five produce $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$. Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average because the matching variable absorbs part of the injection signal. We adopt cross-subject matching as the standard protocol (\\Cref{app:fig_matching}).\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Impact}\n\\label{sec:downstream}\n\nSpearman correlation between full and stable-item rankings is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$: aggregate model ordering is preserved despite 31\\% item instability. DIF-flagged items nonetheless carry more discriminative signal than average ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ vs.\\ random removal across 21 conditions), though a circularity ablation shows DIF-based removal is the \\emph{least} disruptive among non-random strategies (Jaccard overlap with high-discrimination items is only 0.12). Individual model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Llama-3 derivatives gain a median of ${+}53$ rank positions (mean ${+}121$), while Mistral-based models lose a median of $35$ (mean $-54$); individual extremes reach ${+}793$ and $-542$ (\\Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Overall, 25\\% of models shift by $|\\Delta\\mathrm{Rank}| \\geq 100$. \\Cref{sec:gsm8k} demonstrates that this instability pattern extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation}\n\\label{sec:gsm8k}\n\nTemporal DIF extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). C\\% ranges from 17.9\\% (HellaSwag) to 31.3\\% (MMLU), each 45--174$\\times$ above its random-split baseline---none attributable to sampling noise. A task-type dichotomy emerges: knowledge and reasoning benchmarks (MMLU, WinoGrande~\\citep{sakaguchi2021winogrande}, TruthfulQA~\\citep{lin2022truthfulqa}, ARC~\\citep{clark2018arc}) show C\\% $= 26.9$--$31.3\\%$, while procedural benchmarks (GSM8K, HellaSwag~\\citep{zellers2019hellaswag}) show C\\% $= 17.9$--$18.9\\%$, consistent with knowledge-intensive items being more sensitive to shifts in training data composition across model generations.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Temporal DIF across six METABENCH benchmarks. C\\% = ETS~C items ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$) between 2023 and 2024 cohorts. All six show substantial instability (45--174$\\times$ above random baseline), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks exhibiting higher C\\% than procedural ones.}\n\\label{tab:cross_benchmark}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrl}\n\\toprule",

Temporal cohort: logit(p) θ corr=0.9986 with total score, all θ variants FPR=31-35% (no improvement over plan_011 external_5 FPR=31.4%). TPR well maintained (0.83-0.88 at p=0.5). Ability cohort: quantile θ bins (qt_10/qt_20) reduce FPR from 49% to 8-9%, but TPR collapses from 76% to 21% at p=0.5 — proving plan_011 ability cohort's high TPR was mainly ability confounding, not genuine DIF detection. The key additions I need to make are a clear statement that MH-DIF identifies that items function differently but not why they do, which should go in the methods or discussion, and a note that the semi-synthetic framework validates detection sensitivity rather than interpretation of the observed DIF.

I'll execute these changes section by section: start with the code release footnote, then move through the contamination language edits across multiple sections, add the new paragraph clarifying what DIF means, expand the protocol discussion, adjust the ConStat visibility in the experiments section, compress the intro to create space, and finally compile everything.

    "oldString": "The MH-DIF procedure is well-established in psychometrics~\\citep{holland1988mh,clauser1998dif}; the cross-subject matching strategy has psychometric precedent~\\citep{nandakumar1993dif}. Our contributions are therefore empirical and methodological, not algorithmic: (a)~the first large-scale DIF characterization across six LLM benchmarks (27,125 items, 5,227 models), finding 17.9--31.3\\% temporal instability robust across thresholds and unrelated to accuracy gains (confirmed by Simpson's paradox in odds ratio regression); (b)~a ground truth meta-critique exposing that web-overlap labels are poorly suited to evaluate item-level differential detectors; (c)~a reusable semi-synthetic validation framework with cross-subject matching that addresses LLM-specific challenges (multi-domain local dependence handling and simultaneous contamination control, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$), confirmed on GSM8K; and (d)~a concrete DIF audit protocol for benchmark maintenance (\\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}). We make the following contributions:",
    "newString": "The MH-DIF procedure is well-established in psychometrics~\\citep{holland1988mh,clauser1998dif}; the cross-subject matching strategy has psychometric precedent~\\citep{nandakumar1993dif}. Our contributions are empirical and methodological, not algorithmic:",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWhen we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (MH-DIF) analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, and find that 31\\% of MMLU~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$), indicating large shifts in item functioning between models released in 2023 and 2024. This is not statistical noise: a random-split control within the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\\% ETS~C items. The instability persists across all 57 MMLU subjects (C\\% range 21--48\\%, unweighted mean $\\approx 30\\%$), and item-level regression shows that 92.4\\% of this instability is unrelated to aggregate domain-level accuracy gains. Nearly one in three MMLU items behaves differently depending on when the evaluated models were trained. Crucially, this instability does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between model rankings computed on all items versus stable-only items is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$. The instability is diagnostic, not destructive; it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations rather than rendering MMLU scores unreliable.\n\nA growing body of contamination detection research has produced sophisticated item-level methods, from membership inference approaches such as Min-K\\%~\\citep{shi2024mink} to performance-based detectors such as ConStat~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat}, DVD~\\citep{liang2026dvd}, and Self-Critique~\\citep{tao2026selfcritique}. These methods validate detection accuracy primarily against web-overlap labels such as those of \\citet{li2024contamination}, which classify benchmark items as contaminated based on verbatim or near-verbatim matches in publicly accessible training corpora. Such labels measure \\emph{global exposure}: whether an item exists in the data ecosystem that all training pipelines can access. They do not measure \\emph{differential item functioning}: whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts that may have had varying degrees of exposure or different training methodologies. \\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential} illustrates the distinction. Most MMLU items are globally contaminated in the sense that they exist in web-accessible text, so all model cohorts encounter them and no differential signal arises. The items that function differentially are those where exposure or training methodology changed asymmetrically across cohorts. Validating differential detectors against global-exposure labels therefore conflates two phenomena. Direct comparison confirms near-independence between the two flag sets (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$), consistent across contamination subtypes.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_1_global_vs_differential.pdf}\n\\caption{Global vs.\\ differential contamination, illustrated via Item Characteristic Curves (ICCs). \\textbf{Left}: global contamination produces no DIF; both cohorts encounter the item, and their ICCs overlap. \\textbf{Right}: differential contamination produces DIF; the focal cohort's ICC shifts because only that cohort was disproportionately exposed during training. The enrichment ratio of $\\approx 1.0$ between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is expected because most MMLU items fall into the left category.}\n\\label{fig:global_vs_differential}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe apply classical MH-DIF with ETS classification to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge, the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks. This psychometric approach complements concurrent work on anchor-item calibration for score comparability~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addresses the fragility of existing contamination detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Semi-synthetic injection experiments confirm that DIF detects differential contamination when present ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$), and our cross-subject matching protocol eliminates false positive inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$). Three structural controls (matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning, and within-subject pre-checks) confirm that the 31\\% temporal instability cannot be attributed to methodological artifacts, establishing it as a robust empirical finding about LLM benchmark measurement properties, replicated across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\\Cref{sec:gsm8k}).\n\nMMLU's 12,508 items provide sufficient measurement redundancy that removing nearly a third does not alter rank order ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$). DIF should therefore be understood as a diagnostic lens rather than a quality filter. DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal: removing them degrades ranking discriminability more than random removal ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ across 21 conditions). Model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Llama-3 derivatives gain a median of ${+}53$ rank positions under stable-item evaluation, while Mistral-based models lose a median of $35$; 25\\% of models shift by $|\\Delta\\mathrm{Rank}| \\geq 100$.\n\nThe MH-DIF procedure is well-established in psychometrics~\\citep{holland1988mh,clauser1998dif}; the cross-subject matching strategy has psychometric precedent~\\citep{nandakumar1993dif}. Our contributions are therefore empirical and methodological, not algorithmic: (a)~the first large-scale DIF characterization across six LLM benchmarks (27,125 items, 5,227 models), finding 17.9--31.3\\% temporal instability robust across thresholds and unrelated to accuracy gains (confirmed by Simpson's paradox in odds ratio regression); (b)~a ground truth meta-critique exposing that web-overlap labels are poorly suited to evaluate item-level differential detectors; (c)~a reusable semi-synthetic validation framework with cross-subject matching that addresses LLM-specific challenges (multi-domain local dependence handling and simultaneous contamination control, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$), confirmed on GSM8K; and (d)~a concrete DIF audit protocol for benchmark maintenance (\\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}). We make the following contributions:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Benchmark temporal comparability} (Claim 1): 31\\% of MMLU items are functionally unstable across model generations under ETS~C classification; replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks yields C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$ (45--174$\\times$ above random baselines), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks showing higher instability than procedural ones (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:gsm8k}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Ground truth meta-critique} (Claim 2): Web-overlap contamination labels~\\citep{li2024contamination} do not provide appropriate ground truth for item-level differential detectors. Direct empirical comparison on 9,229 items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$), exposing a validation methodology gap in the contamination detection literature (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Semi-synthetic validation framework} (Claim 3): Controlled injection with cross-subject matching achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$); cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K confirms generality (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic,sec:gsm8k}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{DIF as diagnostic lens} (Claim 4): DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative information ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ vs.\\ random removal, Jaccard overlap with high-discrimination items $= 0.12$) and exhibit a structured difficulty--direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$): capability gains concentrate on hard items while easy items erode (\\Cref{sec:downstream}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (MH-DIF) analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, and find that 31\\% of MMLU~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$), indicating large shifts in item functioning between models released in 2023 and 2024. This is not statistical noise: a random-split control within the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\\% ETS~C items. The instability persists across all 57 MMLU subjects (C\\% range 21--48\\%, unweighted mean $\\approx 30\\%$), and item-level regression shows that 92.4\\% of this instability is unrelated to aggregate domain-level accuracy gains. Nearly one in three MMLU items behaves differently depending on when the evaluated models were trained. Crucially, this instability does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between model rankings computed on all items versus stable-only items is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$. The instability is diagnostic, not destructive; it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations rather than rendering MMLU scores unreliable.\n\nA growing body of contamination detection research has produced sophisticated item-level methods, from membership inference approaches such as Min-K\\%~\\citep{shi2024mink} to performance-based detectors such as ConStat~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat}, DVD~\\citep{liang2026dvd}, and Self-Critique~\\citep{tao2026selfcritique}. These methods validate detection accuracy primarily against web-overlap labels such as those of \\citet{li2024contamination}, which classify benchmark items as contaminated based on verbatim or near-verbatim matches in publicly accessible training corpora. Such labels measure \\emph{global exposure}: whether an item exists in the data ecosystem that all training pipelines can access. They do not measure \\emph{differential item functioning}: whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts that may have had varying degrees of exposure or different training methodologies. \\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential} illustrates the distinction. Most MMLU items are globally contaminated in the sense that they exist in web-accessible text, so all model cohorts encounter them and no differential signal arises. The items that function differentially are those where exposure or training methodology changed asymmetrically across cohorts. Validating differential detectors against global-exposure labels therefore conflates two phenomena. The enrichment ratio of $\\approx 1.0$ between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is expected because most MMLU items fall into the left category.}\n\\label{fig:global_vs_differential}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe apply classical MH-DIF with ETS classification to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge, the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks. This psychometric approach complements concurrent work on anchor-item calibration for score comparability~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addresses the fragility of existing contamination detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Semi-synthetic injection experiments confirm that DIF detects differential contamination when present ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$), and our cross-subject matching protocol eliminates false positive inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$). Three structural controls (matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning, and within-subject pre-checks) confirm that the 31\\% temporal instability cannot be attributed to methodological artifacts, establishing it as a robust empirical finding about LLM benchmark measurement properties, replicated across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\\Cref{sec:gsm8k}).\n\nMMLU's 12,508 items provide sufficient measurement redundancy that removing nearly a third does not alter rank order ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$). DIF should therefore be understood as a diagnostic lens rather than a quality filter. DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal: removing them degrades ranking discriminability more than random removal ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ across 21 conditions).      ```latex
     \subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation}
     \label{sec:gsm8k}
     
     Temporal DIF extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). C\% ranges from 17.9\% (HellaSwag) to 31.3\% (MMLU), each 45--174$\times$ above its random-split baseline---none attributable to sampling noise. A task-type dichotomy emerges: knowledge and reasoning benchmarks (MMLU, WinoGrande~\citep{sakaguchi2021winogrande}, TruthfulQA~\citep{lin2022truthfulqa}, ARC~\citep{clark2018arc}) show C\% $= 26.9$--$31.3\%$, while procedural benchmarks (GSM8K, HellaSwag~\citep{zellers2019hellaswag}) show C\% $= 17.9$--$18.9\%$, consistent with knowledge-intensive items being more sensitive to shifts in training data composition across model generations.
     
     \begin{table}[t]
     ...6-row table with MMLU/WinoGrande/TruthfulQA/ARC/GSM8K/HellaSwag...
     \end{table}
     
     On GSM8K specifically, semi-synthetic injection with external matching confirms detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$ at $p = 0.5$; \Cref{app:gsm8k_details}). An exploratory GSM1K analysis ($N = 33$) shows a negative correlation between contamination gap and DIF ranking advantage ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$; partial $r = -0.385$, $p = 0.027$), suggesting DIF captures capability change distinct from memorization (\Cref{app:fig_gsm1k}).
     ```
     - §4.2 Simpson's paradox addition (appended after "92.4% of instability is item-specific"):
     ```latex
     Odds ratio regression reveals a Simpson's paradox: domain accuracy improvement positively predicts DIF at the aggregate level ($\mathrm{OR} = 1.55$ per 10 pp, $p = 0.001$) but reverses after controlling for item difficulty and discrimination ($\mathrm{OR} = 0.73$, $p = 0.030$; LR $\chi^2 = 647$), confirming that item properties---not accuracy gains---drive DIF classification.
     ```

   - `paper/sections/abstract.tex`
     - Updated scope to 6 benchmarks with knowledge/procedural dichotomy and Simpson's paradox. Current state:
     ```latex
     \begin{abstract}
     LLM benchmark scores are routinely compared across model generations, yet the assumption that individual items measure the same construct over time is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (DIF) analysis with ETS severity classification to six METABENCH benchmarks (5,227 models, 27,125 total items), and find that 17.9--31.3\% of items exhibit large DIF (ETS Category~C) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts---knowledge/reasoning benchmarks show higher instability (C\% $\geq 26.9\%$) than procedural ones (C\% $\leq 18.9\%$), each 45--174$\times$ above random baselines. This does not threaten benchmark validity ($\rho \approx 0.997$ between full and stable-item rankings) but reveals which capability dimensions shift across generations. Item-level decomposition shows 92.4\% of instability is item-specific; a Simpson's paradox in odds ratio regression confirms item properties, not accuracy gains, drive DIF. We expose a validation methodology gap: web-overlap labels measure global exposure, not differential functioning (enrichment $\approx 0.96$). A semi-synthetic injection framework with cross-subject matching preserves detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$) with zero false positive inflation. DIF-flagged items concentrate cross-generational discriminative information ($z = -8\sigma$ to $-49\sigma$ vs.\ random removal), and a difficulty--direction interaction ($\rho = -0.597$) reveals that capability gains concentrate on hard items while easy items erode, suggesting DIF captures genuine capability change rather than noise. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting.
     \end{abstract}
     ```

     \subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}
     \label{sec:disc_protocol}
     
     These findings motivate a concrete benchmark maintenance protocol requiring only a binary response matrix and temporal cohort labels. We recommend: (1)~quarterly MH-DIF audits (or whenever $N \geq 80$ new models appear), using cross-subject matching; (2)~benchmark-wide C\% $> 20\%$ triggers item review, with items flagged ETS~C in consecutive audits labeled ``unstable''; (3)~graduated responses: flag-and-annotate, item rotation, or weighted scoring.
