     Applying MH-DIF analysis to six METABENCH benchmarks (27,125 items, 5,227 models), we find 17.9--31.3\% temporal item instability under ETS~C classification---knowledge/reasoning benchmarks are more affected (C\% $\geq 26.9\%$) than procedural ones (C\% $\leq 18.9\%$). The instability is confirmed non-random (random controls $\leq 0.6\%$) and item-specific; a Simpson's paradox in odds ratio regression confirms item properties, not accuracy gains, drive DIF. Web-overlap labels show near-independence with DIF flags (enrichment $\approx 0.96$); our semi-synthetic framework ($\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$, $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$) provides a controlled alternative.
     
     DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative information ($z = -8\sigma$ to $-49\sigma$ vs.\ random removal) and exhibit a difficulty--direction interaction ($\rho = -0.597$): capability gains on hard items, erosion on easy ones. DIF is a diagnostic lens, not a quality filter. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring alongside score reporting.

        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWhen we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (MH-DIF) analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench},\\footnote{Analysis code and DIF audit scripts are available at \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/dif-audit}.} and find that 31\\% of MMLU~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$), indicating large shifts in item functioning between models released in 2023 and 2024. This is not statistical noise: a random-split control within the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\\% ETS~C items. The instability persists across all 57 MMLU subjects (C\\% range 21--48\\%, unweighted mean $\\approx 30\\%$), and item-level regression shows that 92.4\\% of this instability is unrelated to aggregate domain-level accuracy gains. Nearly one in three MMLU items behaves differently depending on when the evaluated models were trained. Crucially, this instability does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between model rankings computed on all items versus stable-only items is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$. The instability is diagnostic, not destructive; it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations rather than rendering MMLU scores unreliable.\n\nA growing body of contamination detection research has produced sophisticated item-level methods, from membership inference approaches such as Min-K\\%~\\citep{shi2024mink} to performance-based detectors such as ConStat~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat}, DVD~\\citep{liang2026dvd}, and Self-Critique~\\citep{tao2026selfcritique}. These methods validate detection accuracy primarily against web-overlap labels such as those of \\citet{li2024contamination}, which classify benchmark items as contaminated based on verbatim or near-verbatim matches in publicly accessible training corpora. Such labels measure \\emph{global exposure}: whether an item exists in the data ecosystem that all training pipelines can access. They do not measure \\emph{differential item functioning}: whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts that may have had varying degrees of exposure or different training methodologies. \\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential} illustrates the distinction. Most MMLU items are globally contaminated in the sense that they exist in web-accessible text, so all model cohorts encounter them and no differential signal arises. The items that function differentially are those where exposure or training methodology changed asymmetrically across cohorts. Validating differential detectors against global-exposure labels therefore conflates two phenomena. Direct comparison confirms near-independence between the two flag sets (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$), consistent across contamination subtypes.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_1_global_vs_differential.pdf}\n\\caption{Global vs.\\ differential contamination, illustrated via Item Characteristic Curves (ICCs). \\textbf{Left}: global contamination produces no DIF; both cohorts encounter the item, and their ICCs overlap. \\textbf{Right}: differential contamination produces DIF; the focal cohort's ICC shifts because only that cohort was disproportionately exposed during training. The enrichment ratio of $\\approx 1.0$ between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is expected because most MMLU items fall into the left category.}\n\\label{fig:global_vs_differential}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe apply classical MH-DIF with ETS classification to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge, the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks. This psychometric approach complements concurrent work on anchor-item calibration for score comparability~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addresses the fragility of existing contamination detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Semi-synthetic injection experiments confirm that DIF detects differential contamination when present ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$), and our cross-subject matching protocol eliminates false positive inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$). Three structural controls (matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning, and within-subject pre-checks) confirm that the 31\\% temporal instability cannot be attributed to methodological artifacts, establishing it as a robust empirical finding about LLM benchmark measurement properties, replicated across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\\Cref{sec:gsm8k}).\n\nMMLU's 12,508 items provide sufficient measurement redundancy that removing nearly a third does not alter rank order ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$). DIF should therefore be understood as a diagnostic lens rather than a quality filter. DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal: removing them degrades ranking discriminability more than random removal ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ across 21 conditions). Model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Llama-3 derivatives gain a median of ${+}53$ rank positions under stable-item evaluation, while Mistral-based models lose a median of $35$; 25\\% of models shift by $|\\Delta\\mathrm{Rank}| \\geq 100$.\n\nThe MH-DIF procedure is well-established in psychometrics~\\citep{holland1988mh,clauser1998dif}; the cross-subject matching strategy has psychometric precedent~\\citep{nandakumar1993dif}. Our contributions are empirical and methodological, not algorithmic:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Benchmark temporal comparability} (Claim 1): 31\\% of MMLU items are functionally unstable across model generations under ETS~C classification; replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks yields C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$ (45--174$\\times$ above random baselines), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks showing higher instability than procedural ones (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:gsm8k}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Ground truth meta-critique} (Claim 2): Web-overlap contamination labels~\\citep{li2024contamination} do not provide appropriate ground truth for item-level differential detectors. Direct empirical comparison on 9,229 items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$), exposing a validation methodology gap in the contamination detection literature (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Semi-synthetic validation framework} (Claim 3): Controlled injection with cross-subject matching achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$); cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K confirms generality (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic,sec:gsm8k}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{DIF as diagnostic lens} (Claim 4): DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative information ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ vs.\\ random removal, Jaccard overlap with high-discrimination items $= 0.12$) and exhibit a structured difficulty--direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$): capability gains concentrate on hard items while easy items erode (\\Cref{sec:downstream}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
        "numLines": 30,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 30
      }

        "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nLLM benchmark scores are routinely compared across model generations, yet the assumption that individual items measure the same construct over time is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (DIF) analysis with ETS severity classification to six METABENCH benchmarks (5,227 models, 27,125 total items), and find that 17.9--31.3\\% of items exhibit large DIF (ETS Category~C) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts---knowledge/reasoning benchmarks show higher instability (C\\% $\\geq 26.9\\%$) than procedural ones (C\\% $\\leq 18.9\\%$), each 45--174$\\times$ above random baselines. This does not threaten benchmark validity ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$ between full and stable-item rankings) but reveals which capability dimensions shift across generations. Item-level decomposition shows 92.4\\% of instability is item-specific; a Simpson's paradox in odds ratio regression confirms item properties, not accuracy gains, drive DIF. We expose a validation methodology gap: web-overlap labels measure global exposure, not differential functioning (enrichment $\\approx 0.96$). A semi-synthetic injection framework with cross-subject matching preserves detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$) with zero false positive inflation. DIF-flagged items concentrate cross-generational discriminative information ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ vs.\\ random removal), and a difficulty--direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$) reveals that capability gains concentrate on hard items while easy items erode, suggesting DIF captures genuine capability change rather than noise. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
        "numLines": 4,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 4
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../paper/sections/abstract.tex"
  },

        "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nApplying MH-DIF analysis to six METABENCH benchmarks (27,125 items, 5,227 models), we find 17.9--31.3\\% temporal item instability under ETS~C classification---knowledge/reasoning benchmarks are more affected (C\\% $\\geq 26.9\\%$) than procedural ones (C\\% $\\leq 18.9\\%$).         "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Implications for Benchmark Temporal Comparability}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\nThe 31\\% ETS~C instability on MMLU is robust across thresholds and structural controls (\\Cref{sec:structural}), and extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$, 45--174$\\times$ above random baselines; \\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). Knowledge/reasoning benchmarks show higher instability (C\\% $\\geq 26.9\\%$) than procedural ones (C\\% $\\leq 18.9\\%$), and a Simpson's paradox in odds ratio regression ($\\mathrm{OR}: 1.55 \\to 0.73$ after controlling for item properties; LR $\\chi^2 = 647$) confirms that item properties---not accuracy gains---drive DIF. This does not invalidate these benchmarks as ranking instruments ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$) but signals measurement drift across model generations.\n\n\\subsection{Ground Truth Meta-Critique}\n\\label{sec:disc_groundtruth}\n\nThe near-independence between DIF flags and web-overlap labels (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$; consistent across contamination subtypes) exposes a category error: web-overlap labels identify a condition shared by nearly all MMLU items (global exposure), so evaluating a differential method against them conflates two phenomena. Item-level differential detectors require item-level differential ground truth.\n\n\\subsection{DIF as Diagnostic Lens}\n\\label{sec:disc_diagnostic}\n\nThe GSM1K correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$, $N = 33$; \\Cref{sec:gsm8k}) provides exploratory evidence that DIF-flagged items capture behavioral change distinct from memorization; partial correlation ($r = -0.385$, $p = 0.027$) after controlling for accuracy confirms this is not an artifact of general model quality.\n\nItem characterization reinforces this: DIF-C items are harder ($d = -0.247$) and more discriminating ($d = 0.230$), distributed uniformly across subjects ($\\chi^2$ $p = 0.32$). A difficulty $\\times$ direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$, $p < 0.001$) shows capability gains concentrate on hard items (88.7\\% focal-favoring in hardest decile) while easy items erode (96.2\\% reference-favoring in easiest decile).\n\nYen's Q3 analysis confirms that local dependence does not drive DIF: extreme LD correlates negligibly with subject-level C\\% ($r = 0.09$, $p = 0.50$), while reasoning-heavy domains with low LD show higher DIF ($r = -0.36$, $p = 0.006$).\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\nThese findings motivate a concrete benchmark maintenance protocol requiring only a binary response matrix and temporal cohort labels. We recommend: (1)~quarterly MH-DIF audits (or whenever $N \\geq 80$ new models appear), using cross-subject matching; (2)~benchmark-wide C\\% $> 20\\%$ triggers item review, with items flagged ETS~C in consecutive audits labeled ``unstable''; (3)~graduated responses: flag-and-annotate, item rotation, or weighted scoring.\n",
        "numLines": 27,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 27
      }
    },

        "content": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Methodological scope of MH-DIF.}\nThe Mantel-Haenszel procedure detects only \\emph{uniform} DIF, where the odds ratio between cohorts is constant across ability strata. Logistic regression DIF analysis reveals an additional 24.8\\% of items exhibiting non-uniform DIF ($|\\beta_{\\mathrm{interaction}}| \\geq 0.5$), making the 31\\% ETS~C estimate conservative. Future work should apply non-uniform DIF methods (e.g., logistic regression or IRT-based approaches) as the primary analysis to capture the full spectrum of item instability. Critically, MH-DIF is nonparametric and conditions on observed total score, not latent $\\theta$---its validity does not depend on IRT model fit~\\citep{holland1988mh}. Our Yen's Q3 analysis confirms that local dependence domains do not correlate with DIF domains ($r = 0.09$, $p = 0.50$), further supporting the robustness of the MH procedure in this setting.\n\n\\paragraph{Ability confounding.}\nAlthough the temporal cohort ability gap is negligible (Cohen's $d = -0.17$, overlap $= 76.4\\%$) and nearest-neighbor matching confirms that at most 2.4 percentage points of C\\% are attributable to ability mismatch, within-family comparisons with large ability gaps (e.g., Llama-2 vs.\\ Llama-3, $d = -0.99$) produce substantially inflated C\\% (61.3\\% unmatched vs.\\ 28.3\\% matched). Any application of MH-DIF to cohorts with larger ability differences must incorporate ability matching or stratification beyond the standard 5-strata procedure.\n\n\\paragraph{Sample size requirements.}\nOur power analysis indicates reliable ETS~C detection requires $N \\geq 80$ per cohort. The temporal cohorts (1,080 and 807 models) far exceed this threshold, but the GSM1K correlation analysis ($N = 33$) has limited statistical power (95\\% CI $= [-0.71, -0.16]$). The negative correlation between contamination gap and DIF ranking advantage ($r = -0.478$) is exploratory and warrants replication with larger model samples.\n\n\\paragraph{Contamination vs.\\ capability change.}\nDIF analysis detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts but cannot determine \\emph{why}. The differential signal may reflect contamination, capability change, training methodology shifts, or interactions among these factors. We present evidence that DIF captures something distinct from memorization (negative GSM1K correlation, semi-synthetic dose-response), but a definitive causal decomposition remains out of reach without access to model training data.\n\n\\paragraph{Benchmark scope.}\nWe analyze six METABENCH benchmarks (MMLU, WinoGrande, TruthfulQA, ARC, GSM8K, HellaSwag; 27,125 total items), all multiple-choice or short-answer benchmarks scored as binary correct/incorrect. The generalizability to open-ended generation benchmarks (e.g., AlpacaEval, MT-Bench), coding benchmarks (e.g., HumanEval), or benchmarks with non-binary scoring remains untested. The binary response format is a prerequisite for MH-DIF; extending to continuous or partial-credit scoring would require polytomous DIF methods.\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal cohort definition.}\nWe define cohorts by calendar year of model release (2023 vs.\\ 2024), a coarse grouping that aggregates models with diverse training pipelines, data mixtures, and post-training procedures. Finer-grained cohort definitions (e.g., by training data cutoff, model family, or alignment method) might reveal more targeted patterns of item instability but would reduce per-cohort sample sizes and statistical power.\n",
        "numLines": 20,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 20
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../paper/sections/limitations.tex"
  },

1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We apply MH-DIF to the METABENCH response matrix~\citep{kipnis2024metabench} (5,227 models $\times$ 12,508 MMLU items~\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}), with temporal cohorts defined by release date (2023 vs.\ 2024). Cross-benchmark validation across all six METABENCH benchmarks appears in \Cref{sec:gsm8k}.
5	
6	\subsection{Validation: DIF Detection Power}
7	\label{sec:validation}
8	
9	A power analysis using empirical MMLU IRT parameters confirms that at $N \geq 80$ per cohort, ETS~C achieves TPR of 0.68--0.80, FPR of 0.036--0.042, and AUC of 0.93--0.94 for items with $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.4$. Our temporal cohorts ($N_{\mathrm{ref}} = 1{,}080$, $N_{\mathrm{focal}} = 807$) far exceed this threshold.
10	
11	\subsection{Temporal Comparability: 31\% Item Instability}
12	\label{sec:temporal}
13	
14	We compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$). Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$ removes only 3 items (31.32\% $\to$ 31.30\%), because the dual ETS threshold on effect size and significance already provides implicit multiple-testing protection.
15	
16	Three controls confirm this reflects genuine temporal change. A random split of the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\% ETS~C items (sampling noise), and an ability-quintile comparison within the same temporal cohort yields 2.9\% (ability differences alone). A within-family comparison (Llama-2 vs.\ Llama-3) produces 61.3\% ETS~C items, elevated by large ability gaps that confound the DIF signal~\citep{holland1988mh}. The contrast between 2.9\% (ability-controlled) and 61.3\% (ability-confounded) brackets the temporal rate of 31.3\%. Crucially, the temporal cohort ability gap is negligible: Cohen's $d = -0.17$ (below the $|d| = 0.2$ small-effect threshold) with 76.4\% distributional overlap. Under strict 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching ($d \approx -0.010$, $N = 638$ per group, 5 seeds), C\% $= 28.9\% \pm 0.1\%$---a 7.7\% relative reduction from 31.3\%, confirming that at most $\sim$2.4 pp of observed DIF is attributable to ability mismatch.
17	
18	Across all 57 MMLU subjects, C\% ranges from 20.7\% to 48.4\% (mean $\approx 30\%$); within-subject matching on 5 subjects yields 38.1\% as a robustness check (\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}). Item-level logistic regression shows domain accuracy improvement explains only 2.4 percentage points of 31.3\% C\% (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$); 92.4\% of instability is item-specific. Odds ratio regression reveals a Simpson's paradox: domain accuracy improvement positively predicts DIF at the aggregate level ($\mathrm{OR} = 1.55$ per 10 pp, $p = 0.001$) but reverses after controlling for item difficulty and discrimination ($\mathrm{OR} = 0.73$, $p = 0.030$; LR $\chi^2 = 647$), confirming that item properties---not accuracy gains---drive DIF classification. The conclusion is robust across thresholds: C\% $= 48.9\%$ at $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.0$, $18.3\%$ at $\geq 2.0$, $10.2\%$ at $\geq 2.5$.
19	
20	\begin{table}[t]
21	\centering
22	\caption{DIF landscape across cohort definitions. ETS~C denotes the percentage of items with $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$. FPR is measured against Li et al.\ web-overlap labels on the ``clean'' (non-overlapping) subset. Enrichment is the ratio of observed to expected overlap between DIF flags and web-overlap labels.}
23	\label{tab:temporal_landscape}
24	\small
25	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
26	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
27	\toprule
28	Cohort Definition & $N_{\mathrm{ref}}$ / $N_{\mathrm{focal}}$ & C\% & FPR (clean) & Enrich. \\
29	\midrule
30	Temporal (2023 vs.\ 2024) & 1,080 / 807 & 31.3 & 0.318 & 0.964 \\
31	Ability quintile (Q1--2 vs.\ Q4--5) & ${\sim}$2,600 / ${\sim}$2,600 & 2.9 & 0.027 & 1.19 \\
32	Within-family (Llama-2 vs.\ 3) & 161 / 308 & 61.3 & -- & -- \\
33	Random split (sanity) & 540 / 540 & 0.18 & -- & -- \\
34	Within-subject (avg, 5 subj.) & \textit{varies} & 38.1 & -- & -- \\
35	\bottomrule
36	\end{tabular}}
37	\end{table}
38	
39	\subsection{Ground Truth Mismatch}
40	\label{sec:groundtruth}
41	
42	Direct comparison of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \citet{li2024contamination} on 9,229 annotated items confirms that the two methods capture orthogonal signals. The enrichment ratio is 0.964 (\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): DIF-C rates are 30.7\% among web-contaminated items and 31.8\% among clean items. Association tests yield $\phi = 0.012$, $\chi^2 = 1.28$ ($p = 0.258$), and OR $= 1.05$ [0.96, 1.15]; the Jaccard index between flagged sets is 0.206. This near-independence holds across contamination subtypes (input-only enrichment $= 0.986$, input-and-label $= 0.960$). The null result does not indicate detection failure: semi-synthetic injection produces a clear dose-response ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \Cref{fig:dose_response}). The disconnect arises because web-overlap labels measure \emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training corpora), while DIF measures \emph{differential functioning} (whether an item's relationship to ability changes across cohorts). The Li et al.\ labels are the wrong ground truth for a differential method.
43	
44	\begin{figure}[t]
45	\centering
46	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_2_dose_response.pdf}
47	\caption{Semi-synthetic dose-response for DIF detection on MMLU. $\Delta\mathrm{TPR}$ increases monotonically with exploitation rate $p$, confirming that MH-DIF detects differential contamination when present. Baseline (no injection) produces $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} \approx 0$.}
48	\label{fig:dose_response}
49	\end{figure}
50	
51	\subsection{Structural Analysis}
52	\label{sec:structural}
53	
54	Three structural controls fail to reduce temporal C\% below 31\%: matched-ability quintiles, IRT $\theta$-conditioning ($r(\theta, \mathrm{total\ score}) = 0.9986$, FPR $= 0.312$), and within-subject DIF (C\% $= 38.1\%$, exceeding the 25\% bifactor viability threshold). Logistic regression DIF confirms 92.1\% concordance with MH and reveals additional non-uniform DIF (24.8\% at $|\beta_{\mathrm{interaction}}| \geq 0.5$), making the 31\% estimate conservative. Graduated de-duplication ($K{=}10$: 22.7\% vs.\ random 26.8\%; $K{=}20$: 29.1\% vs.\ 30.9\%) confirms family structure does not inflate C\%. Ability-matched within-family analysis reduces Llama-2 vs.\ Llama-3 C\% from 61.3\% to $28.3\% \pm 1.6\%$ (Cohen's $d = -0.99$, overlap $= 0.18$), while temporal matching reduces C\% from 31.3\% to $28.9\% \pm 0.1\%$ ($d = -0.17$, overlap $= 0.76$). The matched C\% values ($\sim$28--29\%) are consistent with each other despite vastly different unmatched rates (61.3\% vs.\ 31.3\%), suggesting that genuine item-level DIF accounts for $\sim$28--29\% of items, with the Llama comparison carrying 33 pp of additional ability confound (54\% reduction) versus only 2.4 pp for temporal cohorts (7.7\% reduction). Full details in \Cref{app:structural}.
55	
56	\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Framework Validation}
57	\label{sec:semisynthetic}
58	
59	The critical methodological finding is that the choice of matching strategy determines validity. Cross-subject matching computes the conditioning variable from subjects \emph{other than} the one being tested, achieving $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects while four of five produce $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$. Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average because the matching variable absorbs part of the injection signal. We adopt cross-subject matching as the standard protocol (\Cref{app:fig_matching}).
60	
61	\subsection{Downstream Impact}
62	\label{sec:downstream}
63	
64	Spearman correlation between full and stable-item rankings is $\rho \approx 0.997$: aggregate model ordering is preserved despite 31\% item instability. DIF-flagged items nonetheless carry more discriminative signal than average ($z = -8\sigma$ to $-49\sigma$ vs.\ random removal across 21 conditions), though a circularity ablation shows DIF-based removal is the \emph{least} disruptive among non-random strategies (Jaccard overlap with high-discrimination items is only 0.12). Individual model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Llama-3 derivatives gain a median of ${+}53$ rank positions (mean ${+}121$), while Mistral-based models lose a median of $35$ (mean $-54$); individual extremes reach ${+}793$ and $-542$ (\Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Overall, 25\% of models shift by $|\Delta\mathrm{Rank}| \geq 100$. \Cref{sec:gsm8k} demonstrates that this instability pattern extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks.
65	
66	\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation}
67	\label{sec:gsm8k}
68	
69	Temporal DIF extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). C\% ranges from 17.9\% (HellaSwag) to 31.3\% (MMLU), each 45--174$\times$ above its random-split baseline---none attributable to sampling noise. A task-type dichotomy emerges: knowledge and reasoning benchmarks (MMLU, WinoGrande~\citep{sakaguchi2021winogrande}, TruthfulQA~\citep{lin2022truthfulqa}, ARC~\citep{clark2018arc}) show C\% $= 26.9$--$31.3\%$, while procedural benchmarks (GSM8K, HellaSwag~\citep{zellers2019hellaswag}) show C\% $= 17.9$--$18.9\%$, consistent with knowledge-intensive items being more sensitive to shifts in training data composition across model generations.
70	
71	\begin{table}[t]
72	\centering
73	\caption{Temporal DIF across six METABENCH benchmarks. C\% = ETS~C items ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$) between 2023 and 2024 cohorts. All six show substantial instability (45--174$\times$ above random baseline), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks exhibiting higher C\% than procedural ones.}
74	\label{tab:cross_benchmark}
75	\small
76	\begin{tabular}{lrrrl}
77	\toprule
78	Benchmark & Items & C\% & Rand.\ C\% & Ratio \\
79	\midrule
80	MMLU & 12{,}508 & 31.3 & 0.18 & 174$\times$ \\
81	WinoGrande & 1{,}267 & 30.3 & 0.32 & 95$\times$ \\
82	TruthfulQA & 817 & 29.7 & 0.37 & 80$\times$ \\
83	ARC & 1{,}172 & 26.9 & 0.60 & 45$\times$ \\
84	GSM8K & 1{,}319 & 18.9 & 0.30 & 63$\times$ \\
85	HellaSwag & 10{,}042 & 17.9 & 0.28 & 64$\times$ \\
86	\bottomrule
87	\end{tabular}
88	\end{table}
89	
90	On GSM8K specifically, semi-synthetic injection with external matching confirms detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$ at $p = 0.5$; \Cref{app:gsm8k_details}). An exploratory GSM1K analysis ($N = 33$) shows a negative correlation between contamination gap and DIF ranking advantage ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$; partial $r = -0.385$, $p = 0.027$), suggesting DIF captures capability change distinct from memorization (\Cref{app:fig_gsm1k}).
91	
```

  "old_string": "These findings motivate a concrete benchmark maintenance protocol requiring only a binary response matrix and temporal cohort labels. We recommend: (1)~quarterly MH-DIF audits (or whenever $N \\geq 80$ new models appear), using cross-subject matching; (2)~benchmark-wide C\\% $> 20\\%$ triggers item review, with items flagged ETS~C in consecutive audits labeled ``unstable''; (3)~graduated responses: flag-and-annotate, item rotation, or weighted scoring.",
  "new_string": "These findings motivate a concrete benchmark maintenance protocol requiring only a binary response matrix and temporal cohort labels. We recommend: (1)~MH-DIF audits whenever $\\geq 500$ new models accumulate on a leaderboard (or quarterly, whichever comes first), using cross-subject matching to avoid FPR inflation (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}); (2)~benchmark-wide C\\% $> 20\\%$ triggers item review---items flagged ETS~C in two consecutive audits are labeled ``unstable,'' while items that flip between flagged and unflagged across audits (``flip-flop'' items) receive a separate ``volatile'' tag indicating stochastic sensitivity rather than systematic drift; (3)~graduated responses: flag-and-annotate (attach DIF metadata to leaderboard scores), item rotation (replace unstable items with newly validated alternatives), or weighted scoring (down-weight ETS~C items in aggregate computation). As a worked example: MMLU's current C\\% $= 31.3\\%$ exceeds the 20\\% threshold, triggering review of the 3{,}915 ETS~C items; a benchmark maintainer would first check whether these items cluster in specific subjects (they do not: $\\chi^2$ $p = 0.32$), then apply graduated response starting with annotation."
}

    "newString": "These findings motivate a concrete benchmark maintenance protocol requiring only a binary response matrix and temporal cohort labels. We recommend: (1)~MH-DIF audits whenever $\\geq 500$ new models accumulate on a leaderboard (or quarterly, whichever comes first), using cross-subject matching to avoid FPR inflation (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}); (2)~benchmark-wide C\\% $> 20\\%$ triggers item review---items flagged ETS~C in two consecutive audits are labeled ``unstable,'' while items that flip between flagged and unflagged across audits (``flip-flop'' items) receive a separate ``volatile'' tag indicating stochastic sensitivity rather than systematic drift; (3)~graduated responses: flag-and-annotate (attach DIF metadata to leaderboard scores), item rotation (replace unstable items with newly validated alternatives), or weighted scoring (down-weight ETS~C items in aggregate computation). As a worked example: MMLU's current C\\% $= 31.3\\%$ exceeds the 20\\% threshold, triggering review of the 3{,}915 ETS~C items; a benchmark maintainer would first check whether these items cluster in specific subjects (they do not: $\\chi^2$ $p = 0.32$), then apply graduated response starting with annotation.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Implications for Benchmark Temporal Comparability}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\nThe 31\\% ETS~C instability on MMLU is robust across thresholds and structural controls (\\Cref{sec:structural}), and extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$, 45--174$\\times$ above random baselines; \\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). Knowledge/reasoning benchmarks show higher instability (C\\% $\\geq 26.9\\%$) than procedural ones (C\\% $\\leq 18.9\\%$), and a Simpson's paradox in odds ratio regression ($\\mathrm{OR}: 1.55 \\to 0.73$ after controlling for item properties; LR $\\chi^2 = 647$) confirms that item properties---not accuracy gains---drive DIF. This does not invalidate these benchmarks as ranking instruments ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$) but signals measurement drift across model generations.\n\n\\subsection{Ground Truth Meta-Critique}\n\\label{sec:disc_groundtruth}\n\nThe near-independence between DIF flags and web-overlap labels (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$; consistent across contamination subtypes) exposes a category error: web-overlap labels identify a condition shared by nearly all MMLU items (global exposure), so evaluating a differential method against them conflates two phenomena. Item-level differential detectors require item-level differential ground truth.\n\n\\subsection{DIF as Diagnostic Lens}\n\\label{sec:disc_diagnostic}\n\nThe GSM1K correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$, $N = 33$; \\Cref{sec:gsm8k}) provides exploratory evidence that DIF-flagged items capture behavioral change distinct from memorization; partial correlation ($r = -0.385$, $p = 0.027$) after controlling for accuracy confirms this is not an artifact of general model quality.\n\nItem characterization reinforces this: DIF-C items are harder ($d = -0.247$) and more discriminating ($d = 0.230$), distributed uniformly across subjects ($\\chi^2$ $p = 0.32$). A difficulty $\\times$ direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$, $p < 0.001$) shows capability gains concentrate on hard items (88.7\\% focal-favoring in hardest decile) while easy items erode (96.2\\% reference-favoring in easiest decile).\n\nYen's Q3 analysis confirms that local dependence does not drive DIF: extreme LD correlates negligibly with subject-level C\\% ($r = 0.09$, $p = 0.50$), while reasoning-heavy domains with low LD show higher DIF ($r = -0.36$, $p = 0.006$).\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\nThese findings motivate a concrete benchmark maintenance protocol requiring only a binary response matrix and temporal cohort labels. We recommend: (1)~quarterly MH-DIF audits (or whenever $N \\geq 80$ new models appear), using cross-subject matching; (2)~benchmark-wide C\\% $> 20\\%$ triggers item review, with items flagged ETS~C in consecutive audits labeled ``unstable''; (3)~graduated responses: flag-and-annotate, item rotation, or weighted scoring.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nLLM benchmark scores are routinely compared across model generations, yet the assumption that individual items measure the same construct over time is rarely tested. This does not threaten benchmark validity ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$ between full and stable-item rankings) but reveals which capability dimensions shift across generations. Item-level decomposition shows 92.4\\% of instability is item-specific; a Simpson's paradox in odds ratio regression confirms item properties, not accuracy gains, drive DIF. We expose a validation methodology gap: web-overlap labels measure global exposure, not differential functioning (enrichment $\\approx 0.96$). A semi-synthetic injection framework with cross-subject matching preserves detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$) with zero false positive inflation. DIF-flagged items concentrate cross-generational discriminative information ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ vs.\\ random removal), and a difficulty--direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$) reveals that capability gains concentrate on hard items while easy items erode, suggesting DIF captures genuine capability change rather than noise. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting.\n\\end{abstract}\n",

    "oldString": "Semi-synthetic injection experiments confirm that DIF detects differential contamination when present ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$)",
    "newString": "Semi-synthetic injection experiments confirm that MH-DIF is sensitive to differential item behavior when present ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$)",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWhen we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (MH-DIF) analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench},\\footnote{Analysis code and DIF audit scripts are available at \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/dif-audit}.} and find that 31\\% of MMLU~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$), indicating large shifts in item functioning between models released in 2023 and 2024. This is not statistical noise: a random-split control within the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\\% ETS~C items. The instability persists across all 57 MMLU subjects (C\\% range 21--48\\%, unweighted mean $\\approx 30\\%$), and item-level regression shows that 92.4\\% of this instability is unrelated to aggregate domain-level accuracy gains. Nearly one in three MMLU items behaves differently depending on when the evaluated models were trained. Crucially, this instability does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between model rankings computed on all items versus stable-only items is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$. The instability is diagnostic, not destructive; it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations rather than rendering MMLU scores unreliable.\n\nA growing body of contamination detection research has produced sophisticated item-level methods, from membership inference approaches such as Min-K\\%~\\citep{shi2024mink} to performance-based detectors such as ConStat~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat}, DVD~\\citep{liang2026dvd}, and Self-Critique~\\citep{tao2026selfcritique}. These methods validate detection accuracy primarily against web-overlap labels such as those of \\citet{li2024contamination}, which classify benchmark items as contaminated based on verbatim or near-verbatim matches in publicly accessible training corpora. Such labels measure \\emph{global exposure}: whether an item exists in the data ecosystem that all training pipelines can access. They do not measure \\emph{differential item functioning}: whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts that may have had varying degrees of exposure or different training methodologies. \\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential} illustrates the distinction. Most MMLU items are globally contaminated in the sense that they exist in web-accessible text, so all model cohorts encounter them and no differential signal arises. The items that function differentially are those where exposure or training methodology changed asymmetrically across cohorts. Validating differential detectors against global-exposure labels therefore conflates two phenomena. Direct comparison confirms near-independence between the two flag sets (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$), consistent across contamination subtypes.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_1_global_vs_differential.pdf}\n\\caption{Global vs.\\ differential contamination, illustrated via Item Characteristic Curves (ICCs). \\textbf{Left}: global contamination produces no DIF; both cohorts encounter the item, and their ICCs overlap. \\textbf{Right}: differential contamination produces DIF; the focal cohort's ICC shifts because only that cohort was disproportionately exposed during training. The enrichment ratio of $\\approx 1.0$ between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is expected because most MMLU items fall into the left category.}\n\\label{fig:global_vs_differential}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe apply classical MH-DIF with ETS classification to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge, the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks. This psychometric approach complements concurrent work on anchor-item calibration for score comparability~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addresses the fragility of existing contamination detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Semi-synthetic injection experiments confirm that DIF detects differential contamination when present ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$), and our cross-subject matching protocol eliminates false positive inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$). Three structural controls (matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning, and within-subject pre-checks) confirm that the 31\\% temporal instability cannot be attributed to methodological artifacts, establishing it as a robust empirical finding about LLM benchmark measurement properties, replicated across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\\Cref{sec:gsm8k}).\n\nMMLU's 12,508 items provide sufficient measurement redundancy that removing nearly a third does not alter rank order ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$). DIF should therefore be understood as a diagnostic lens rather than a quality filter. DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal: removing them degrades ranking discriminability more than random removal ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ across 21 conditions). Model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Llama-3 derivatives gain a median of ${+}53$ rank positions under stable-item evaluation, while Mistral-based models lose a median of $35$; 25\\% of models shift by $|\\Delta\\mathrm{Rank}| \\geq 100$.\n\nThe MH-DIF procedure is well-established in psychometrics~\\citep{holland1988mh,clauser1998dif}; the cross-subject matching strategy has psychometric precedent~\\citep{nandakumar1993dif}. Our contributions are empirical and methodological, not algorithmic:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Benchmark temporal comparability} (Claim 1): 31\\% of MMLU items are functionally unstable across model generations under ETS~C classification; replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks yields C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$ (45--174$\\times$ above random baselines), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks showing higher instability than procedural ones (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:gsm8k}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Ground truth meta-critique} (Claim 2): Web-overlap contamination labels~\\citep{li2024contamination} do not provide appropriate ground truth for item-level differential detectors. Direct empirical comparison on 9,229 items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$), exposing a validation methodology gap in the contamination detection literature (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Semi-synthetic validation framework} (Claim 3): Controlled injection with cross-subject matching achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$); cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K confirms generality (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic,sec:gsm8k}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{DIF as diagnostic lens} (Claim 4): DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative information ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ vs.\\ random removal, Jaccard overlap with high-discrimination items $= 0.12$) and exhibit a structured difficulty--direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$): capability gains concentrate on hard items while easy items erode (\\Cref{sec:downstream}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

Crucially, MH-DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}---the differential signal may reflect contamination, capability change, training methodology shifts, or interactions among these factors.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWhen we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (MH-DIF) analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench},\\footnote{Analysis code and DIF audit scripts are available at \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/dif-audit}.} and find that 31\\% of MMLU~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$), indicating large shifts in item functioning between models released in 2023 and 2024. This is not statistical noise: a random-split control within the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\\% ETS~C items. The instability persists across all 57 MMLU subjects (C\\% range 21--48\\%, unweighted mean $\\approx 30\\%$), and item-level regression shows that 92.4\\% of this instability is unrelated to aggregate domain-level accuracy gains. Nearly one in three MMLU items behaves differently depending on when the evaluated models were trained. Crucially, this instability does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between model rankings computed on all items versus stable-only items is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$. The instability is diagnostic, not destructive; it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations rather than rendering MMLU scores unreliable.\n\nA growing body of contamination detection research has produced sophisticated item-level methods, from membership inference approaches such as Min-K\\%~\\citep{shi2024mink} to performance-based detectors such as ConStat~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat}, DVD~\\citep{liang2026dvd}, and Self-Critique~\\citep{tao2026selfcritique}. These methods validate detection accuracy primarily against web-overlap labels such as those of \\citet{li2024contamination}, which classify benchmark items as contaminated based on verbatim or near-verbatim matches in publicly accessible training corpora. Such labels measure \\emph{global exposure}: whether an item exists in the data ecosystem that all training pipelines can access. They do not measure \\emph{differential item functioning}: whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts that may have had varying degrees of exposure or different training methodologies. \\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential} illustrates the distinction. Most MMLU items are globally contaminated in the sense that they exist in web-accessible text, so all model cohorts encounter them and no differential signal arises. The items that function differentially are those where exposure or training methodology changed asymmetrically across cohorts. Validating differential detectors against global-exposure labels therefore conflates two phenomena. Direct comparison confirms near-independence between the two flag sets (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$), consistent across contamination subtypes.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_1_global_vs_differential.pdf}\n\\caption{Global vs.\\ differential contamination, illustrated via Item Characteristic Curves (ICCs). \\textbf{Left}: global contamination produces no DIF; both cohorts encounter the item, and their ICCs overlap. \\textbf{Right}: differential contamination produces DIF; the focal cohort's ICC shifts because only that cohort was disproportionately exposed during training. The enrichment ratio of $\\approx 1.0$ between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is expected because most MMLU items fall into the left category.}\n\\label{fig:global_vs_differential}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe apply classical MH-DIF with ETS classification to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge, the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks. This psychometric approach complements concurrent work on anchor-item calibration for score comparability~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addresses the fragility of existing contamination detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Semi-synthetic injection experiments confirm that MH-DIF is sensitive to differential item behavior when present ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$), and our cross-subject matching protocol eliminates false positive inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$). Three structural controls (matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning, and within-subject pre-checks) confirm that the 31\\% temporal instability cannot be attributed to methodological artifacts, establishing it as a robust empirical finding about LLM benchmark measurement properties, replicated across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\\Cref{sec:gsm8k}).\n\nMMLU's 12,508 items provide sufficient measurement redundancy that removing nearly a third does not alter rank order ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$). DIF should therefore be understood as a diagnostic lens rather than a quality filter. DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal: removing them degrades ranking discriminability more than random removal ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ across 21 conditions). Model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Llama-3 derivatives gain a median of ${+}53$ rank positions under stable-item evaluation, while Mistral-based models lose a median of $35$; 25\\% of models shift by $|\\Delta\\mathrm{Rank}| \\geq 100$.\n\nThe MH-DIF procedure is well-established in psychometrics~\\citep{holland1988mh,clauser1998dif}; the cross-subject matching strategy has psychometric precedent~\\citep{nandakumar1993dif}. Our contributions are empirical and methodological, not algorithmic:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Benchmark temporal comparability} (Claim 1): 31\\% of MMLU items are functionally unstable across model generations under ETS~C classification; replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks yields C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$ (45--174$\\times$ above random baselines), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks showing higher instability than procedural ones (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:gsm8k}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Ground truth meta-critique} (Claim 2): Web-overlap contamination labels~\\citep{li2024contamination} do not provide appropriate ground truth for item-level differential detectors. Direct empirical comparison on 9,229 items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$), exposing a validation methodology gap in the contamination detection literature (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Semi-synthetic validation framework} (Claim 3): Controlled injection with cross-subject matching achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$); cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K confirms generality (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic,sec:gsm8k}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{DIF as diagnostic lens} (Claim 4): DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative information ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ vs.\\ random removal, Jaccard overlap with high-discrimination items $= 0.12$) and exhibit a structured difficulty--direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$): capability gains concentrate on hard items while easy items erode (\\Cref{sec:downstream}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

  "replace_all": false,

  "old_string": "\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}",
  "new_string": "\\subsection{From Demographic Fairness to Temporal Measurement Invariance}\n\\label{sec:disc_transplant}\n\nIn educational testing, DIF analysis compares demographic groups (e.g., gender, ethnicity) to detect items that disadvantage a subgroup after controlling for ability---a fairness diagnostic~\\citep{holland1988mh,clauser1998dif}. Transplanting this framework to LLM evaluation redefines the grouping variable: cohorts are temporal (2023 vs.\\ 2024 models) rather than demographic, and the construct of interest shifts from \\emph{fairness} to \\emph{measurement invariance over time}. This reframing preserves the statistical machinery---MH odds ratios, ETS severity classification, cross-subject matching---while changing what the flags mean. A DIF-flagged item in education signals potential bias; in LLM evaluation, it signals that the item's relationship to overall ability has shifted across model generations. For benchmark maintainers, the practical implication is that temporal DIF provides a lightweight, assumption-free diagnostic (requiring only a binary response matrix and cohort labels) that can be integrated into existing leaderboard infrastructure without IRT model fitting or access to training data.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}"
}
```

    "oldString": "\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}",
    "newString": "\\subsection{From Demographic Fairness to Temporal Measurement Invariance}\n\\label{sec:disc_transplant}\n\nIn educational testing, DIF analysis compares demographic groups (e.g., gender, ethnicity) to detect items that disadvantage a subgroup after controlling for ability---a fairness diagnostic~\\citep{holland1988mh,clauser1998dif}. Transplanting this framework to LLM evaluation redefines the grouping variable: cohorts are temporal (2023 vs.\\ 2024 models) rather than demographic, and the construct of interest shifts from \\emph{fairness} to \\emph{measurement invariance over time}. This reframing preserves the statistical machinery---MH odds ratios, ETS severity classification, cross-subject matching---while changing what the flags mean. A DIF-flagged item in education signals potential bias; in LLM evaluation, it signals that the item's relationship to overall ability has shifted across model generations. For benchmark maintainers, the practical implication is that temporal DIF provides a lightweight, assumption-free diagnostic (requiring only a binary response matrix and cohort labels) that can be integrated into existing leaderboard infrastructure without IRT model fitting or access to training data.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Implications for Benchmark Temporal Comparability}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\nThe 31\\% ETS~C instability on MMLU is robust across thresholds and structural controls (\\Cref{sec:structural}), and extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$, 45--174$\\times$ above random baselines; \\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). Knowledge/reasoning benchmarks show higher instability (C\\% $\\geq 26.9\\%$) than procedural ones (C\\% $\\leq 18.9\\%$), and a Simpson's paradox in odds ratio regression ($\\mathrm{OR}: 1.55 \\to 0.73$ after controlling for item properties; LR $\\chi^2 = 647$) confirms that item properties---not accuracy gains---drive DIF. This does not invalidate these benchmarks as ranking instruments ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$) but signals measurement drift across model generations.\n\n\\subsection{Ground Truth Meta-Critique}\n\\label{sec:disc_groundtruth}\n\nThe near-independence between DIF flags and web-overlap labels (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$; consistent across contamination subtypes) exposes a category error: web-overlap labels identify a condition shared by nearly all MMLU items (global exposure), so evaluating a differential method against them conflates two phenomena. Item-level differential detectors require item-level differential ground truth.\n\n\\subsection{DIF as Diagnostic Lens}\n\\label{sec:disc_diagnostic}\n\nThe GSM1K correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$, $N = 33$; \\Cref{sec:gsm8k}) provides exploratory evidence that DIF-flagged items capture behavioral change distinct from memorization; partial correlation ($r = -0.385$, $p = 0.027$) after controlling for accuracy confirms this is not an artifact of general model quality.\n\nItem characterization reinforces this: DIF-C items are harder ($d = -0.247$) and more discriminating ($d = 0.230$), distributed uniformly across subjects ($\\chi^2$ $p = 0.32$). A difficulty $\\times$ direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$, $p < 0.001$) shows capability gains concentrate on hard items (88.7\\% focal-favoring in hardest decile) while easy items erode (96.2\\% reference-favoring in easiest decile).\n\nYen's Q3 analysis confirms that local dependence does not drive DIF: extreme LD correlates negligibly with subject-level C\\% ($r = 0.09$, $p = 0.50$), while reasoning-heavy domains with low LD show higher DIF ($r = -0.36$, $p = 0.006$).\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\nThese findings motivate a concrete benchmark maintenance protocol requiring only a binary response matrix and temporal cohort labels. We recommend: (1)~MH-DIF audits whenever $\\geq 500$ new models accumulate on a leaderboard (or quarterly, whichever comes first), using cross-subject matching to avoid FPR inflation (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}); (2)~benchmark-wide C\\% $> 20\\%$ triggers item review---items flagged ETS~C in two consecutive audits are labeled ``unstable,'' while items that flip between flagged and unflagged across audits (``flip-flop'' items) receive a separate ``volatile'' tag indicating stochastic sensitivity rather than systematic drift; (3)~graduated responses: flag-and-annotate (attach DIF metadata to leaderboard scores), item rotation (replace unstable items with newly validated alternatives), or weighted scoring (down-weight ETS~C items in aggregate computation). As a worked example: MMLU's current C\\% $= 31.3\\%$ exceeds the 20\\% threshold, triggering review of the 3{,}915 ETS~C items; a benchmark maintainer would first check whether these items cluster in specific subjects (they do not: $\\chi^2$ $p = 0.32$), then apply graduated response starting with annotation.\n",

  "new_string": "The null result does not indicate detection failure: semi-synthetic injection produces a clear dose-response ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \\Cref{fig:dose_response}). The disconnect arises because web-overlap labels measure \\emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training corpora), while DIF measures \\emph{differential functioning} (whether an item's relationship to ability changes across cohorts). The Li et al.\\ labels are the wrong ground truth for a differential method.
